**Africa** is the world's second-largest and second-most populous [continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continent) after [Asia](689.md). At about 30.3 million km<sup>2</sup> (11.7 million square miles) including adjacent islands, it covers around 20% of [Earth](9228.md)'s land area and 6% of its total surface area.[^cite_note-Sayre-9] With nearly 1.4 billion of the [world population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_population), [Africa's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Africa) is the youngest among the continents;[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11] the median age in 2012 was 19.7, when the worldwide median age was 30.4.[^cite_note-12] Based on 2024 projections, Africa's population will exceed 3.8 billion people by 2100.[^cite_note-13] Africa is the least wealthy inhabited continent per capita and second-least wealthy by total wealth, ahead of [Oceania](22621.md). Scholars have attributed this to various factors including [geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Africa), [climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Africa),[^cite_note-Africa_Slow-14] corruption,[^cite_note-Africa_Slow-14] [colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa), the [Cold War](325329.md),[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16] and [neocolonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neocolonialism). Despite this low concentration of wealth, the [African economy](1055517.md) is home to many of the [world's fastest-growing economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_real_GDP_growth_rate).

Africa straddles the [equator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equator) and the [prime meridian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_meridian). The continent is surrounded by the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the north, the [Arabian plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_plate) and the [Gulf of Aqaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Aqaba) to the northeast, the [Indian Ocean](14580.md) to the southeast and the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) to the west. [France](5843419.md), [Italy](14532.md), [Portugal](23033.md), [Spain](26667.md), and [Yemen](350939.md) have parts of their [territories located on African geographical soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_in_Africa), mostly in the form of islands.

The continent includes [Madagascar](18964.md) and various archipelagos. It contains [54 fully recognised sovereign states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_in_Africa), eight cities and islands that are part of non-African states, and two *de facto* independent [states with limited or no recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_with_limited_recognition). This count does not include [Malta](19137.md) and [Sicily](27619.md), which are geologically part of the African continent. [Algeria](358.md) is the largest country by area, and [Nigeria](21383.md) is largest by population. Nations cooperate through the establishment of the [African Union](10634933.md), which is headquartered in [Addis Ababa](52593.md).

Africa is highly biodiverse;[^cite_note-17] it is the continent with the largest number of [megafauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megafauna) species, as it was least affected by the [extinction of the Pleistocene megafauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Pleistocene_extinctions#Introduction). However, it is [heavily affected by a wide range of environmental issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Africa), including desertification, deforestation, water scarcity, and pollution. These entrenched environmental concerns are expected to worsen as [climate change impacts Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Africa). The UN [Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change](15030.md) has identified Africa as the continent most [vulnerable to climate change](66204363.md).[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19]

The [history of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Africa) is long, complex, and varied, and has often been under-appreciated by the global [historical community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_historiography).[^cite_note-20] In [African societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indigenous_peoples#Africa) the oral word is revered, and they have generally recorded their history via oral tradition, which has led anthropologists to term them "oral civilisations", contrasted with "literate civilisations" which pride the written word.[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24]<sup>: 142–143 </sup> [African culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Africa) is rich and diverse both within and across [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_regions_of_Africa), encompassing [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_art), [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_cuisine), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Africa) and [dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_dance), [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Africa), and [dress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing_in_Africa).&#x20;

Africa, particularly [Eastern Africa](260987.md), is widely accepted to be the place of origin of humans and the [Hominidae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominidae) clade, also known as the great apes. The earliest hominids and their ancestors have been dated to around 7 million years ago, and *[Homo sapiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens)* (modern human) are believed to have originated in Africa 350,000 to 260,000 years ago.[^cite_note-31] Throughout history the continent saw the rise and fall of [many kingdoms and empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_kingdoms_and_empires_in_African_history), such as [Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), [Makuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makuria), the [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate), [Mali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mali_Empire), the [Asante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asante_Empire), [Kongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kongo), the [Luba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luba_Empire), [Bunyoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunyoro), [Great Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Zimbabwe), [Imerina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imerina), and the [Zulu](14754726.md). Despite the predominance of states, many societies were [heterarchical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterarchy) and [stateless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stateless_society).[^cite_note-34] [Slave trades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery) created [various diasporas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora), especially [in the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora_in_the_Americas). From the late-19th century to early 20th century, driven by the [Second Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Industrial_Revolution), most of Africa was [rapidly conquered and colonised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa) by [European nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_empire), save for [Ethiopia](187749.md) and [Liberia](17791.md).[^cite_note-35] European rule had [significant impacts on Africa's societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analysis_of_European_colonialism_and_colonization), and colonies were maintained for the purpose of economic exploitation and extraction of natural resources. Most present states emerged from [a process of decolonisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation_of_Africa) following [World War II](32927.md) and established the [Organisation of African Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_African_Unity) in 1963, the predecessor to the African Union.[^cite_note-Hargreaves_1996-36] The nascent countries decided to keep their colonial borders, with [traditional power structures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_current_non-sovereign_African_monarchs) used in governance to varying degrees.

## Etymology



[Equal Earth projection](58246920.md) map of Africa, 2018

*[Afri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afri)* was a Latin name used to refer to the inhabitants of what was then known as northern Africa, located west of the [Nile](21244.md) river, and in its widest sense referring to all lands south of the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean), also known as [Ancient Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Libya).[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] This name seems to have originally referred to a native Libyan tribe, an ancestor of modern [Berbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berbers);[^cite_note-39] see [Terence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terence#Biography) for discussion. The name had usually been connected with the [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenician_language) word *_ʿafar_* meaning "dust",[^cite_note-40] but a 1981 hypothesis[^cite_note-41] has asserted that it stems from the [Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_languages) word *ifri* (plural *ifran*) meaning "cave", in reference to cave dwellers.[^cite_note-Michell-42] The same word[^cite_note-Michell-42] may be found in the name of the [Banu Ifran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Ifran) from [Algeria](358.md) and [Tripolitania](664792.md), a Berber tribe originally from [Yafran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yafran) (also known as *Ifrane*) in northwestern [Libya](17633.md),[^cite_note-43] as well as [Ifrane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifrane), [Morocco](19291.md).

Under [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) rule, [Carthage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthage) became the capital of the province *[Africa Proconsularis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa_\(Roman_province\))*, following the Roman victory over the [Carthaginians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Carthage) in the [Third Punic War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Punic_War) in 146 BC, which also included the coastal part of modern Libya.[^cite_note-44] The Latin suffix *[-ica](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/-ica#Latin)* can sometimes be used to denote a land (e.g., in *[Celtica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallia_Celtica)* from *[Celtae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtae)*, as used by [Julius Caesar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Caesar)). The medieval Muslim region of [Ifriqiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifriqiya), following its conquest of the [Byzantine (Eastern Roman)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) Empire's *[Exarchatus Africae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exarchate_of_Africa)*, preserved a form of the name. According to the Romans, Africa lies to the west of [Egypt](8087628.md), while "Asia" was used to refer to [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) and lands to the east. In his *[Geographia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_\(Ptolemy\)),* [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy) draws a definite line between the two continents by, indicating [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) along the [prime meridian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_meridian) and making the [Isthmus of Suez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isthmus_of_Suez) and the [Red Sea](26191.md) the boundary between Asia and Africa. As Europeans came to understand the real extent of the continent, the idea of "Africa" expanded with their knowledge.

Other etymological hypotheses have been postulated:

- 1st-century Jewish historian [Flavius Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flavius_Josephus) (*Ant. 1.15*) asserted that it was named for [Epher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epher), grandson of [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham) according to [Genesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Genesis) 25\:4, whose descendants, he claimed, had invaded Libya.

- [Isidore of Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isidore_of_Seville) in his 7th-century *[Etymologiae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etymologiae)* XIV.5.2. suggests it comes from the Latin *aprica*, meaning "sunny".

- Massey, in 1881, stated it is derived from the Egyptian *af-rui-ka*, meaning "to turn toward the opening of the Ka". The [ka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_soul#Ka) is the energetic double of every person, and the "opening of the Ka" refers to a womb or birthplace. Africa would be, for the Egyptians, "the birthplace".

  [^cite_note-45]

- Michèle Fruyt in 1976 proposed

  [^cite_note-46]

  &#x20;linking the Latin word with *africus* "south wind", which would be of [Umbrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbrian_language) origin and mean originally "rainy wind".

- Robert R. Stieglitz of [Rutgers University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutgers_University) in 1984 proposed: "The name Africa, derived from the Latin *\*Aphir-ic-a*, is cognate to Hebrew [Ophir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ophir) \['rich']."

  [^cite_note-47]

- [Ibn Khallikan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Khallikan) and some other historians claim the name came from a [Himyarite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite) king called Afrikin ibn Kais ibn Saifi ("Afrikus son of Abraham") who subdued Ifriqiya.

  [^cite_note-48]

  [^cite_note-49]

  [^cite_note-50]

- Arabic *afrīqā* (feminine noun) and *ifrīqiyā*, now usually pronounced *afrīqiyā* (feminine) 'Africa', from *'afara* \[' = *'ain*, not *'alif*] 'to be dusty' from *'afar* 'dust, powder' and *'afir* 'dried, dried up by the sun, withered' and *'affara* 'to dry in the sun on hot sand' or 'to sprinkle with dust'.

  [^cite_note-51]

- Possibly [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenician_language) *faraqa* in the sense of 'colony, separation'.

  [^cite_note-52]

The terms "[North Africa](21714.md)" and "[Sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md)" have been subject to recent criticism from some scholars due to its historical root in [colonialist discourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racialization) which arbitrarily separated Africa into a white "northern" Africa and black "southern" Africa, overlooking [common genetic links](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_Africa) and regional diversity across the continent.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55]

## History

Main article: [History of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Africa)

See also: [History of North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_North_Africa), [History of West Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_West_Africa), [History of Central Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Central_Africa), [History of East Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_East_Africa), [History of Southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Southern_Africa), and [List of kingdoms in Africa throughout history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_kingdoms_in_Africa_throughout_history)

In accordance with [African cosmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_traditional_religions), African historical consciousness viewed historical change and continuity, order and purpose within the framework of man and his environment, the gods, and his ancestors, and he believed himself part of a holistic spiritual entity.[^cite_note-56] In African societies, the historical process is largely a [communal one](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_communalism), with eyewitness accounts, hearsay, reminiscences, and occasionally visions, dreams, and hallucinations crafted into narrative oral traditions which are performed and transmitted through generations.[^cite_note-57]<sup>: 12 </sup><sup>: 48 </sup>

In oral traditions time is sometimes mythical and social, and ancestors were considered historical actors.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-Hama-1981-58]<sup>: 43–53 </sup> Mind and memory shapes traditions, as events are condensed over time and crystallise into clichés.[^cite_note-60]<sup>: 11 </sup>Oral tradition can be [exoteric](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/exoteric) or [esoteric](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/esoteric). It speaks to people according to their understanding, unveiling itself in accordance with their aptitudes.[^cite_note-61]<sup>: 168 </sup> In [African epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_philosophy), the epistemic subject "experiences the epistemic object in a sensuous, emotive, intuitive, abstractive understanding, rather than through abstraction alone, as is the case in Western epistemology" to arrive at a "complete knowledge", and as such oral traditions, [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Africa), proverbs, and the like were used in the preservation and transmission of knowledge.[^cite_note-62]

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistoric Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Africa)

See also: [Recent African origin of modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recent_African_origin_of_modern_humans), [African humid period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_humid_period), and [Sahara pump theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahara_pump_theory)

Africa is considered by most [paleoanthropologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleoanthropology) to be the [oldest inhabited territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cradle_of_Humankind) on Earth, with the Human species originating from the continent.[^cite_note-63] During the mid-20th century, anthropologists discovered many fossils and evidence of human occupation perhaps as early as seven million years ago ("[Before Present](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_Present)"; BP). Fossil remains of several species of early apelike humans thought to have evolved into modern humans, such as *[Australopithecus afarensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australopithecus_afarensis)* [radiometrically dated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiometrically_dated) to approximately 3.9–3.0 million BP,[^cite_note-64] *[Paranthropus boisei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paranthropus_boisei)* (c. 2.3–1.4 million BP)[^cite_note-65] and *[Homo ergaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_ergaster)* (c. 1.9 million–600,000 BP) have been discovered.[^cite_note-Sayre-9]



Successive dispersals of   *[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)* (yellow),   *[Homo neanderthalensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_neanderthalensis)* (ochre) during *[Out of Africa I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out_of_Africa_I)* and   *[Homo sapiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens)* (red, *[Out of Africa II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out_of_Africa_II)*), with the numbers of years since they appeared before present.

After the evolution of *[Homo sapiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens)* approximately 350,000 to 260,000 BP, the continent was mainly populated by groups of [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer).[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] These first modern humans left Africa and populated the rest of the globe during the [Out of Africa II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recent_African_origin_of_modern_humans) migration dated to approximately 50,000 years BP, exiting either across [Bab-el-Mandeb](62138.md) over the Red Sea,[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] the [Strait of Gibraltar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Gibraltar),[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71] or the Isthmus of Suez.[^cite_note-72]

Other migrations of modern humans have been dated to that time, with evidence of early human settlement found in [southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa), [Southeast Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Africa), [North Africa](21714.md), and the [Sahara](325363.md).[^cite_note-73] During the [African humid period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_humid_period), estimated to have been around 10,500 BP, the Sahara had again become a green fertile valley, and its populations returned from the interior and coastal highlands, with [rock art paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saharan_rock_art) depicting a fertile Sahara and large populations discovered in [Tassili n'Ajjer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tassili_n'Ajjer) dating back perhaps 10 millennia.[^cite_note-74] However, the warming and drying climate meant that by 5,000 BP, the Sahara region was becoming increasingly dry and hostile. Around 3500 BC, due to a tilt in [Earth's orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_orbit), the Sahara experienced a period of rapid desertification.[^cite_note-75] The domestication of cattle preceded agriculture and seems to have existed alongside hunter-gatherer cultures. It is speculated that by 6,000 BP, cattle were domesticated in North Africa.[^cite_note-76]

In [West Africa](67393.md), a wet phase ushered in an expanding [rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainforest) and wooded [savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savanna) from Senegal to Cameroon. Between 9,000 and 5,000 BP, [Niger–Congo speakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger%E2%80%93Congo_languages) domesticated the [oil palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elaeis_guineensis) and [raffia palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raffia_palm). [Black-eyed peas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-eyed_pea) and [voandzeia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voandzeia) (African groundnuts) were domesticated, followed by [okra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okra) and [kola nuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kola_nut). Since most of the plants grew in the forest, the Niger–Congo speakers invented polished stone axes for clearing forest.[^cite_note-77]<sup>: 82–4 </sup> [Pygmies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Pygmies) are thought to have inhabited [Central Africa](539606.md) for many millennia, splitting into eastern and western groups around 5,000 BP.[^cite_note-78] Over 150,000 BP, there was an early dispersal of [anatomically modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_human) to Southern Africa, equated with the modern-day [Khoisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoisan) who have preserved their traditional hunter-gatherer way of life.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-HS-25]

### 4th millennium BC – 6th century AD

See also: [Ancient Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Africa) and [History of Africa § 4th millennium BC – 6th century AD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Africa#4th_millennium_BC_%E2%80%93_6th_century_AD)

#### Northeast Africa



Map of [Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), showing its major cities and sites, c. 3150 BC to 30 BC

From 3500 BC, [nomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nome_\(Egypt\)) (ruled by [nomarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomarch)) coalesced to form the kingdoms of [Lower Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Egypt) and [Upper Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Egypt) in [northeast Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Africa). Around 3100 BC Upper Egypt conquered Lower Egypt to unify [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) under the [1st dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Dynasty_of_Egypt), with the process of consolidation and assimilation completed by the time of the [3rd dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3rd_dynasty) who formed the [Old Kingdom of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Kingdom_of_Egypt) in 2686 BC.[^cite_note-Abu_Bakr-1981-81]<sup>: 62–63 </sup> The [Kingdom of Kerma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kerma) emerged around this time to become the dominant force in [Nubia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nubia), controlling territory as large as Egypt between the first and fourth [cataracts of the Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cataracts_of_the_Nile).[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83]

The height of the Old Kingdom saw the construction of many [great pyramids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_pyramids), though under the [6th dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Dynasty_of_Egypt) power gradually decentralised to the nomarchs, culminating in the [disintegration of the kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intermediate_Period_of_Egypt), exacerbated by drought and famine. Around 2055 BC, the [11th dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleventh_Dynasty_of_Egypt), based in [Thebes](65811.md), conquered the others to form the [Middle Kingdom of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Kingdom_of_Egypt), and the [12th dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelfth_Dynasty_of_Egypt) expanded into [Lower Nubia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Nubia) at the expense of Kerma.[^cite_note-Abu_Bakr-1981-81]<sup>: 68–71 </sup> Around 1700 BC, the Middle Kingdom [fractured in two](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Intermediate_Period_of_Egypt), and the [Hyksos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyksos) (a militaristic people from [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\))) invaded and conquered Lower Egypt, while Kerma coordinated invasions deep into Egypt to reach its greatest extent.[^cite_note-84] In 1550 BC, the [18th dynasty](28830228.md) expelled the Hyksos, and established the [New Kingdom of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Kingdom_of_Egypt). The New Kingdom conquered the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) from the [Canaanites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanites), [Mittani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mittani), [Amorites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amorites), and [Hittites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittites), and extinguished Kerma, incorporating Nubia into the empire, and sending the Egyptian empire into its golden age.[^cite_note-Abu_Bakr-1981-81]<sup>: 73 </sup> Internal struggles, drought, famine, and invasions by a [confederation of seafaring peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_peoples) contributed to the New Kingdom's [collapse in 1069 BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Intermediate_Period_of_Egypt).[^cite_note-Abu_Bakr-1981-81]<sup>: 76–77 </sup>

The New Kingdom's collapse liberated the [Kingdom of Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kush) in Nubia, which manoeuvred into power in Upper Egypt and conquered Lower Egypt in 754 BC to form the [Kushite Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-fifth_Dynasty_of_Egypt). The Kushites ruled for a century and oversaw a [revival in pyramid building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nubian_pyramids), until they were [driven out of Egypt by the Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_conquest_of_Egypt) in 663 BC in reprisal for their expansion towards the [Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Empire).[^cite_note-85] The Assyrians [installed a puppet dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-sixth_Dynasty_of_Egypt) that later gained independence and once more [unified Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Period_of_Egypt), until it was conquered by the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) in 525 BC.[^cite_note-Abu_Bakr-1981-81]<sup>: 77 </sup>

Egypt briefly regained independence from the Achaemenids under the [28th dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-eighth_Dynasty_of_Egypt) from 404 to 343 BC. The conquest of [Achaemenid Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Persian_Egypt) by [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) in 332 BC marked the beginning of [Hellenistic rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period) and the installation of the [Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Macedonians) [Ptolemaic dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_dynasty).[^cite_note-86]<sup>: 119 </sup> The Ptolemaics lost their holdings outside of Africa to the [Seleucids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_dynasty) in the [Syrian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Wars), expanded into [Cyrenaica](75451.md), and briefly occupied [part of Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triakontaschoinos) in the 3rd century BC.[^cite_note-87]<sup>: 384–93 </sup> In the 1st century BC, Ptolemaic Egypt became entangled in a [Roman civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesar's_civil_war), leading to its conquest by the [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) in 30 BC.[^cite_note-88] Kush persisted as a major regional power until, having been weakened from internal rebellion amid worsening climatic conditions, invasions by [Aksum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aksum) and the [Noba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noba) caused their disintegration into [Makuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makuria), [Alodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alodia), and [Nobatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobatia) around the 5th century AD.[^cite_note-89]

#### Horn of Africa



[Aksum Obelisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksum_Obelisk), symbol of the Aksumite civilization

In the [Horn of Africa](62142.md), there was the [Land of Punt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_of_Punt), a kingdom on the [Red Sea](26191.md) which was a close trading partner of Ancient Egypt in the 3rd and 2nd millennia BC. Archaeologist [Rodolfo Fattovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodolfo_Fattovich) equates it to the [Gash Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gash_Group) in the Sudanese-Eritrean lowlands, and some scholars have hypothesised modern-day [Somalia](27358.md), while [Kenneth Kitchen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_Kitchen) and [Felix Chami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felix_Chami) locate it on [Zanzibar Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zanzibar_Island).[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91]<sup>: 680 </sup> In the [Eritrean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eritrean_Highlands)-[Ethiopian Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Highlands), the kingdom of [dʿmt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%CA%BFmt) rose c. 980 BC as the region was incorporated into global trading networks,[^cite_note-Fattovich-92] and it exhibited [Sabaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheba) influences which most scholars attribute to a small migration of Sabaeans and their assimilation.[^cite_note-93] Several scholars consider there to have been other contemporaneous states,[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95]<sup>: 39–40 </sup> and dʿmt's collapse in the mid-1st century BC saw the region inhabited by small polities.[^cite_note-Fattovich-92]

Modern-day Somalia was inhabited by [nomadic pastoralists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic_pastoralism), and along the Horn's coast there were many [ancient Somali city-states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Somali_city-states) that thrived off of the [wider Red Sea trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somali_maritime_history) and enjoyed a lucrative monopoly on cinnamon from India due to their freedom from Roman interference.[^cite_note-96]<sup>: 24–5, 33 </sup> In the 1st century AD, the [Kingdom of Aksum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aksum) rose from a city-state to rule much of the northern Ethiopian-Eritrean Highlands and the Red Sea port of [Adulis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adulis). Aksum was described as one of the four great powers by Persian prophet [Mani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mani_\(prophet\)) in the 3rd century.[^cite_note-Phillips-2016-97][^cite_note-98]<sup>: 174 </sup> King [Ezana of Axum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezana_of_Axum) converted from [traditional religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aksum#Religion) to [Christianity](5211.md) in the 4th century, gradually followed by the population. In the 6th century, Aksum [conquered South Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksumite_invasion_of_Himyar), though struggled to maintain control over it, and began to gradually lose its dominance over Red Sea trade to Persians and Arabs.[^cite_note-Phillips-2016-97]

#### Northwest Africa



Ruins of Cuicul in [Djémila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dj%C3%A9mila), a Roman town in the ancient province of [Mauretania Caesariensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauretania_Caesariensis)

Northwest Africa (the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb)) was inhabited by [Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berbers) semi-nomadic pastoralists. In the 1st millennium BC, [Phoenician migration and settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenician_settlement_of_North_Africa) came in search for precious metals in the [Gulf of Tunis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tunis).[^cite_note-Warmington-1981-99]<sup>: 442 </sup> This grew into [Ancient Carthage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Carthage) after gaining independence from [Phoenicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicia) in the 6th century BC, and they built an [extensive trading empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Carthage#Independence,_expansion_and_hegemony) with a strict mercantile network.[^cite_note-100] Carthage's collapse and conquest by Rome in the [Punic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punic_Wars) (3rd and 2nd centuries BC) saw [Numidia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numidia) and [Mauretania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauretania) become major powers in the Maghreb.&#x20;

Towards the end of the 2nd century BC Mauretania fought alongside King [Jugurtha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jugurtha) of Numidia against the Romans in the [Jugurthine War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jugurthine_War) after he had usurped the Numidian throne from a Roman ally. Together they inflicted heavy casualties, with the war ending inconclusively when King [Bocchus I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bocchus_I) of Mauretania sold out Jugurtha to the Romans. Around the turn of the millennium, both came under direct Roman rule.[^cite_note-Warmington-1981-99]<sup>: 460–2 </sup> While [Berber traditional religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_traditional_religion) predominated, some converted to Christianity. In the 5th century AD the [Vandals conquered Roman Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandal_conquest_of_Roman_Africa) before the [fall of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Rome), though the province was reconquered by the [Byzantines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) in the [Vandalic War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandalic_War). Large swaths of indigenous peoples regained self-governance in [Masuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauro-Roman_Kingdom) and its [numerous successor polities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romano-Berber_kingdoms) in the Maghreb, including the kingdoms of [Ouarsenis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ouarsenis), [Aurès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_the_Aur%C3%A8s), and [Altava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Altava).[^cite_note-101]<sup>: 495–7, 500–8 </sup>

#### West Africa

In the western [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel) the rise of settled communities occurred largely as a result of the domestication of [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet) and of [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum), and cattle [pastoralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastoralism) began c. 2500 BC. Extensive east-west belts of deserts, [grasslands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Sudanian_savanna), and [forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinean_forest%E2%80%93savanna_mosaic) from north to south were crucial for the moulding of their respective societies and meant that symbiotic trade relations developed in response to the differing environments.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103]<sup>: 79–80 </sup> Beginning around 4000 BC, the [Tichitt culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tichitt_tradition) in modern-day Mauritania and Mali is the oldest known [complexly organised society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complex_society) in West Africa,[^cite_note-Holl-1985-104] while others included the [Kintampo culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kintampo_culture) in modern-day Ghana, the [Nok culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nok_culture) in modern-day Nigeria, and the [Daima culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daima) around [Lake Chad](59314.md).[^cite_note-105]<sup>: 603–12 </sup>

Towards the end of the 3rd century AD, a [wet period](57630.md#Africa_\(West_African_and_Southeast_African\)) in the Sahel created areas for human habitation and exploitation that had not been habitable for the best part of a millennium. The [Ghana Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghana_Empire) (also called *Wagadu*) rose out of the Tichitt culture, growing wealthy following the introduction of the camel to the western Sahel, which revolutionised the [trans-Saharan trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Saharan_trade) that linked their capital and [Aoudaghost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aoudaghost) with [Tahert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahert) and [Sijilmasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sijilmasa) in North Africa.[^cite_note-106] [Soninke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soninke_people) tradition holds that the final founding of Wagadu occurred after [Dinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinga_\(ruler\)) did a deal with [Bida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bida_\(mythology\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), a serpent deity who was guarding a well, to sacrifice one maiden a year in exchange for assurance regarding plenty of rainfall and gold supply.[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-Conrad-1983-107] Based on [large tumuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumulus#Africa) scattered across West Africa dating to this period, several scholars have speculated that there were further simultaneous and preceding states relative to Wagadu.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-Holl-1985-104]

#### Central, Eastern, and Southern Africa

See also: [Bantu expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_expansion)

In the [grasslands of Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_High_Plateau), [Bantu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_languages)-speaking agriculturalists [migrated southward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_expansion) sometime between 5000 BC and 3000 BC. Despite intensive research, the cause of the migrations, and that of the directions taken, is still unclear,[^cite_note-111] however there is consensus that there were multiple dispersal events. Around 1500 BC, Bantu speakers reached central Cameroon. The 'Western Stream' likely followed the coast and the major rivers of the [Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_River) system southwards to reach the southern fringe of the [Congolian Rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congolian_rainforests) around 500 BC (some may have used the sea to circumvent the rainforest). Their arrival coincided with the spread of [iron metallurgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_metallurgy_in_Africa) through Central Africa.&#x20;

Meanwhile, the 'Eastern Stream' travelled either the northern fringe of the rainforest or the [Ubangi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubangi_River) eastwards, and reached just west of [Lake Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Victoria) around 500 BC. While there, Bantu speakers adopted iron metallurgy from [Cushitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cushitic_languages) speakers already present, and coexisted with them.[^cite_note-Bostoen-2018-112][^cite_note-Juwayeyi-2020-110]<sup>: 23–5 </sup> Dispersal from the [Great Lakes region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Great_Lakes) occurred in two more streams. One went west to meet the Western Stream in the DR Congo and Angola, while the other went south and spread across Eastern and Southern Africa.[^cite_note-Juwayeyi-2020-110]<sup>: 26 </sup>

Around the turn of the millennium, Bantu speakers reached central modern-day Tanzania and near [Dar es Salaam](8500.md), before rapidly moving southwards along the coast to reach modern-day [Kwazulu Natal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwazulu_Natal) in South Africa around the 3rd century AD.[^cite_note-Bostoen-2018-112] Throughout this, Bantu speakers displaced, replaced, or intermarried with and absorbed hunter-gatherer and agricultural groups.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-Juwayeyi-2020-110]<sup>: 32 </sup>[^cite_note-114]<sup>: 636 </sup>

### c. 600 – c. 1800

Main article: [Medieval and early modern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_and_early_modern_Africa)

Further information: [History of Africa § c. 600–c. 1800](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Africa#c._600%E2%80%93c._1800)

#### North Africa, the Central Sahel and Sudan, and the Horn

In the 7th and 8th centuries, as part of the [Arab conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) which sought to [spread Islamic rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam),[^cite_note-al-Fasi-1988a-115]<sup>: 56–7 </sup> the [Rashidun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) and [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) caliphates conquered North Africa at the expense of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) and Berber leaders such as [Kusaila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kusaila) and [Kahina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahina).[^cite_note-al-Fasi-1988b-116]<sup>: 47–9 </sup>[^cite_note-117]<sup>: 233–9 </sup> Large numbers of Berber and [Coptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_people) people willingly converted to Islam, and followers of [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions) ('[People of the Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_of_the_Book)') were protected,[^cite_note-al-Fasi-1988a2-118]<sup>: 56–63 </sup> though followers of Berber traditional religion were violently oppressed.[^cite_note-al-Fasi-1988b2-119]<sup>: 46 </sup> In the 8th century the [Berber Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_Revolt) rocked the caliphate, and Berber dynasties took control over the Maghreb,[^cite_note-120]<sup>: 246–51 </sup> while the [Tulunids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulunids) briefly controlled Egypt.[^cite_note-121]<sup>: 172–7 </sup>



The expansion of the [Almoravid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravid_dynasty), a [Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berbers) [Sanhaja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanhaja) dynasty originating in the [Sahara](325363.md).

In the 10th century, the [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate) rose to power in modern-day Tunisia, established a rival caliphate, and [conquered Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_conquest_of_Egypt), before expanding into the [Middle East](19323.md). In the 11th century the [Almoravids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravid_dynasty) conquered the Maghreb and intervened in the Christian [reconquest of Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista).[^cite_note-122]<sup>: 351 </sup> [Saladin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saladin), a Fatimid [vizier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vizier), usurped power in the 12th century and established the [Ayyubid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubid_dynasty) which restored Egyptian prestige,[^cite_note-Baker-2018-123][^cite_note-Garcin-1984-124]<sup>: 372–3 </sup> while the [Almohad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohad_Caliphate) revolution deposed the Almoravids to the east. After Christian gains in Iberia in the 13th century, the Almohads disintegrated into the [Marinids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marinids), [Zayyanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zayyanids), and [Hafsids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafsids) in modern-day Morocco, Algeria, and Tunisia respectively.[^cite_note-125]

In the face of [Mongol expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Syria), [Mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk) generals [seized power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate) in Egypt and expanded into the Middle East.[^cite_note-Garcin-1984-124]<sup>: 377–81 </sup> In the early-16th century, the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) rapidly [conquered North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_wars_in_Africa) (save for Morocco under [Saadi rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saadi_Sultanate)) to counter [Spanish expansionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93Ottoman_wars#Clash_of_interests_in_the_Mediterranean_and_Europe).[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-Cory-2020-129] In the following centuries, over a million Europeans are estimated to have been captured [captured and enslaved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbary_slave_trade) by [Barbary corsairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbary_corsairs).[^cite_note-130] The [Ottoman regencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Eyalets_of_the_Ottoman_Empire_in_Africa) became more independent during the 18th century.[^cite_note-Cory-2020-129]

In the central Sahel around Lake Chad, the [Kanem–Bornu Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanem%E2%80%93Bornu_Empire) ruled from the 6th century, and projected power over the [Hausa Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_Kingdoms).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] To the east during 7th century, [Makuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makuria) conquered [Nobatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobatia) to become the dominant power in the region and [resisted Muslim expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_battle_of_Dongola).[^cite_note-133] They later entered a severe decline following civil war and [Arab migrations to the Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_the_Sudan_region) and had disintegrated by the 15th century, giving rise to the [Funj Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funj_Sultanate).[^cite_note-134] The [Tunjur kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunjur_kingdom)'s collapse in the central Sudan saw the rise of the [Bagirmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Bagirmi), [Wadai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadai_Sultanate), and [Darfur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Darfur) sultanates,[^cite_note-136] which frequently raided the populations to their south for slaves.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138]

In the Horn of Africa, Aksum lost control of the Red Sea, and its isolation from Christendom caused Ethiopian society to introspect and take inspiration from the [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament).[^cite_note-Tamrat-1977-139]<sup>: 108 </sup> Islamised [Beja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beja_kingdoms) invaded the Ethiopian Highlands, forcing Aksum to migrate south, where, according to tradition, Aksum's expansion was met by a [Jewish or traditionalist queen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gudit) who destroyed the state c. 960.[^cite_note-Mekouria-1988-140]<sup>: 563–6 </sup> During the 13th century, hegemony in the Horn was contested by the Christian [Zagwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagwe_dynasty), the Muslims of [Shewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Shewa), and the traditionalist [Damot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Damot);[^cite_note-141]<sup>: 431–2 </sup> the [Solomonic dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomonic_dynasty) overthrew the Zagwe to found the [Ethiopian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Empire), while Shewa was replaced by [Ifat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Ifat).[^cite_note-Tamrat-1977-139]<sup>: 131, 143 </sup> Soon after, Ethiopia [conquered Damot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_Ethiopia#Early_Solomonic_period_to_Zemene_Mesafint) and the Muslim states, entering into a 'golden age'.[^cite_note-142]<sup>: 19–20 </sup>

In the 16th century Muslims united behind [Adal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adal_Sultanate) in *[jihad](16203.md)* to reconquer the Muslim lands and occupy Ethiopia, until, with [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire) assistance, [Ethiopia counter-attacked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Wayna_Daga), and its sovereignty was restored in a reduced state.[^cite_note-Haberland-1992-143]<sup>: 712–5 </sup> The 16th and 17th centuries also saw [Oromo invasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oromo_expansion) into the Horn, weakening Ethiopia further and causing the collapse of Adal, while [Ajuran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajuran_Sultanate) on the east coast also disintegrated.[^cite_note-Mukhtar-2016-144][^cite_note-145]<sup>: 539–44 </sup> Ethiopia briefly consolidated its authority in the late-17th century, though the turn of the century saw [fragmentation and gradually-increasing anarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zemene_Mesafint), which continued throughout the 18th century.[^cite_note-Haberland-1992-143]<sup>: 703 </sup>

#### West Africa



[Yam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yam_\(vegetable\)) festival in the [Ashanti Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashanti_Empire)

The [Ghana Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghana_Empire) formed in the Sahel from between the 2nd and 8th centuries, while from the 7th century the [Gao Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gao_Empire) ruled to its east.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] Almoravid capture of royal [Aoudaghost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aoudaghost) led to Ghana's conversion to Islam in the 11th century,[^cite_note-148] and climatic changes led to Ghana's conquest by its vassal [Sosso Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sosso_Empire) in the 13th century.[^cite_note-\:0-149] Sosso was quickly overthrown by the [Mali Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mali_Empire) who conquered Gao and dominated the trans-Saharan trade.[^cite_note-150] The [Mossi Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mossi_Kingdoms) were established to its south.[^cite_note-151]

The 15th century saw the crumbling of the Mali Empire, with the dominant power in the region becoming the [Songhai Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songhai_Empire) centered on [Gao](1255721.md).[^cite_note-152] Weakened by internal conflict, in 1591 Gao was [conquered by Morocco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moroccan_invasion_of_the_Songhai_Empire), and the political vacuum came to be filled by the [Segou Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9gou_Empire) and several other states.[^cite_note-Abitbol-1992-153]<sup>: 301–7 </sup> The once lucrative trans-Saharan trade gradually lost its prominence to the coastal Atlantic trade with Europeans, and the following centuries saw many droughts, famines, and epidemics.[^cite_note-Abitbol-1992-153]<sup>: 316–9 </sup>

In the [forest regions of West Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinean_forest%E2%80%93savanna_mosaic), various kingdoms and empires flourished, such as the [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) empires of [Ife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ife_Empire) and [Oyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oyo_Empire),[^cite_note-154] the [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people) [Kingdom of Nri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Nri),[^cite_note-155] the [Edo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edo_people) [Kingdom of Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Benin) (famous for [its art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_the_Kingdom_of_Benin)),[^cite_note-156] the [Dagomba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dagomba_people) [Kingdom of Dagbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dagbon),[^cite_note-157] and the [Akan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akan_people) kingdoms of [Bonoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonoman) and [Adanse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adanse).[^cite_note-158] They came into contact with the Portuguese in the 15th century which saw the start of the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md). The [Asante Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asante_Empire) was established in present-day Ghana.[^cite_note-159] Between 1515 and 1800, 8 million Africans were exported in the Atlantic slave trade.[^cite_note-Bulliet_et_al-5-160]

#### Central Africa

In the western [Congo Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_Basin) by the 13th century there were three main confederations of states: the [Seven Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Kingdoms_of_Kongo_dia_Nlaza), [Mpemba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mpemba), and one led by [Vungu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vungu).[^cite_note-\:2-161]<sup>: 24–25 </sup> In the 14th century the [Kingdom of Kongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kongo) emerged and dominated the region, with the [Tio Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tio_Kingdom) to its north and [Mwene Muji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mwene_Muji) to its northeast.[^cite_note-\:2-161] Portuguese arrival in the 15th century saw Kongo's ruler willingly [convert to Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Kongo) and the start of the Atlantic trade, with slaves being the most lucrative export; in the 16th century, [Afonso I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afonso_I_of_Kongo)'s conquests produced many captives for Kongo's [monopoly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monopoly), and the slave trade rapidly grew to meet demand in the [Americas](29833.md).[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-\:2-161]<sup>: 52–3 </sup> Over the course of the late-16th and 17th centuries, the Portuguese sought to conquer [Ndongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ndongo) (allied to [Matamba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Matamba)) in the [Angolan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angolan_Wars);[^cite_note-164] despite fierce resistance from Queen [Nzinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nzinga_of_Ndongo_and_Matamba), and brief [occupation of Luanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Loango-Angola) by the [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_colonial_empire) (allied to Kongo), Portugal controlled most of Ndongo by the 1670s.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-Thornton-2020c-163]<sup>: 91–122 </sup>[^cite_note-167]<sup>: 150–9 </sup>[^cite_note-Thornton-2020d-168]<sup>: 162–88 </sup> The late-17th century saw Kongo [fragment between royal houses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kongo_Civil_War) as several vassals broke away; by the time of Kongo's restoration in 1709, it had ceased being the dominant regional power.[^cite_note-Thornton-2020d-168]<sup>: 199–213 </sup>[^cite_note-169]<sup>: 241–3 </sup> Also during the 17th century, in the [textile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuba_textiles)-producing northeast, the [Boma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boma_Kingdom), [Yaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaka_Kingdom?action=edit\&redlink=1), and possibly the [Kuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuba_Kingdom) and [Pende](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pende_Kingdom?action=edit\&redlink=1) kingdoms broke away from Mwene Muji.[^cite_note-170]



Christian funeral in the [Kingdom of Kongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kongo), c. 1750

In the eastern Congo Basin, farmers, fishermen, and hunters (often Pygmies) built symbiotic relations.[^cite_note-Vansina-1984-171]<sup>: 554 </sup> [Jan Vansina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Vansina) wrote that in the [southern savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Congolian_forest%E2%80%93savanna_mosaic) 'lords of the land' held priestly roles due to their special relationship with the spirits of the land, and held sway over multiple villages, effectively ruling embryonic kingdoms. Accordingly, as lineages grew, authority was opportunistically absorbed or incorporated by force.[^cite_note-Vansina-19842-172]<sup>: 557–8 </sup> The [centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabongo,_Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo) of the [Luba Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luba_Empire) is yet to undergo archaeological research, meaning knowledge about its rise is limited;[^cite_note-173] its foundation is typically dated to the 14th/15th centuries,[^cite_note-Oliver-2001-174]<sup>: 181 </sup> though scholarly estimates range from the 8th to 17th centuries.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-Reefe-1981-176]<sup>: 59 </sup> A [Lunda state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunda_Empire) formed in the [Nkalany Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nkalany_River) (estimates range from c. 1450 to c. 1700),[^cite_note-177] in the 18th century, Lunda rapidly expanded into an extensive commonwealth across the southern savanna, using the institutions of perpetual kinship and positional succession to incorporate peoples.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-Macola-2015-178] Meanwhile, Luba expanded its number of [tributaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary_state), in some cases using its widely acknowledged prestige to intervene in succession disputes and distribute ritually-powerful royal regalia related to *bulopwe* (sacred kingship), in other cases using conquest.[^cite_note-Macola-2015-178][^cite_note-180]<sup>: 108 </sup>

In the northern Great Lakes, the [Empire of Kitara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Kitara) rose around the 11th century, famed for [its oral traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Kitara#Legends). Historian Peter Robertshaw considers its rulers (the [Chwezi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chwezi)) to have been religious leaders who utilised networks of hill-top shrines[^cite_note-182] to project authority.[^cite_note-Robertshaw-2025-181]<sup>: 849–50, 854–6 </sup> Around 1500, severe droughts and famine destabilised the region, occurring again during the early-17th and 18th centuries.[^cite_note-Chrétien-2003b2-183]<sup>: 142–3 </sup> [Luo-speaking peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luo_peoples) migrated from the Upper Nile basin into modern-day Uganda, and a [Biito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biito_clan) dynasty replaced the Chwezi in [Bunyoro-Kitara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunyoro).[^cite_note-184] Over the following centuries, the kingdoms of Bunyoro-Kitara, [Buganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buganda), and [Rwanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Rwanda) struggled for hegemony among others.[^cite_note-185] Throughout the 16th and 17th centuries, Bunyoro raided states to the south such as [Nkore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nkore) and Rwanda. [Mpororo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mpororo?action=edit\&redlink=1) rose and fragmented during the 18th century, while Buganda rapidly expanded at the expense of Bunyoro to become the major power in the region.[^cite_note-Chrétien-2003b-186]<sup>: 147–56 </sup>[^cite_note-187]<sup>: 801–2 </sup>

#### Eastern and Southern Africa

The East African interior was inhabited by pastoralist [Cushitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cushitic_languages) and [Nilotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nilotic_languages) speakers, along with Bantu speakers who continued to expand and adapt to their lands while absorbing the former two;[^cite_note-188]<sup>: 481–94 </sup> the 17th century saw the establishment of [tributary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribute) systems that stabilised the region.[^cite_note-189]<sup>: 829 </sup> On the [Swahili coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swahili_coast) the [Swahili city-states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swahili_city-states) thrived off of the [Indian Ocean trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_trade) and gradually Islamized, giving rise to the [Kilwa Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilwa_Sultanate) from the 10th century.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] In the 15th century, trade routes for Zimbabwean gold shifted north, weakening Kilwa.[^cite_note-Beaujard-2017-192]<sup>: 375 </sup> The Portuguese arrived at the coast soon after and exploited divisions between coastal city-states to gain control over them while utilising a powerful navy.[^cite_note-Salim-19922-193]<sup>: 756–60 </sup> In the 17th and 18th centuries, [Lamu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamu_Archipelago)-Swahili cities rebelled against the Portuguese and allied the [Omani Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omani_Empire) which supplanted Portuguese rule.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-Salim-1992-195]<sup>: 767–70 </sup>

&#x20;Madagascar was settled by [Austronesian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_peoples) between the 5th and 7th centuries, as societies organized at the behest of *[hasina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasina_\(Madagascar\))* and competed over with one another over the [island's estuaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Madagascar) and [bridgeheads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridgehead).[^cite_note-Randrianja-2009-196]<sup>: 43, 48, 52–53 </sup> From around the 15th century, southern Madagascar became integrated into global trade; the [Sakalava Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakalava_Empire) rose to dominate west-coastal trade, accompanied by numerous other states, and the island saw several failed European colonial ventures.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]



Ruins of [Great Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Zimbabwe), which flourished in the 11th to 15th centuries

In Southern Africa, an early kingdom was [Mapungubwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Mapungubwe),[^cite_note-199] which collapsed c. 1300 as [Great Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Zimbabwe), with its monumental dry-stone walls, rose to become the region's main political and economic centre.[^cite_note-Chirikure-2015-200]<sup>: 17–20 </sup>[^cite_note-Huffman-2005-201]<sup>: 30–55 </sup> Following shifting trade routes north to the [Zambezi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambezi) and unclear circumstances, Great Zimbabwe entered into decline and was succeeded by the [Mutapa Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutapa_Empire) and [Kingdom of Butua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Butua) in the 15th century.[^cite_note-202] Mutapa was briefly vassalised by the Portuguese in the 17th century, the latter half of which saw widespread famine and disease, until Changamire Dombo revolted in the 1680s, conquered Butua to found the [Rozvi Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rozvi_Empire), and expelled all Portuguese from the [Zimbabwean plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zimbabwean_plateau).[^cite_note-203] [Maravi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maravi) around [Lake Malawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Malawi) expanded in the 17th century, but the collapse of its succession system in the 18th century precipitated its disintegration.[^cite_note-204]

By the late-1st millennium CE, Bantu-speaking farmers populated most of Southern Africa's cultivable savannas, reaching southwards to the [Kei River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kei_River); they traded goods such as livestock, grain, and metal tools between communities and with hunter-gatherers. Around the 11th century, more agriculturists—who likely brought with them ancestral [Nguni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguni_languages) and [Sotho-Tswana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sotho%E2%80%93Tswana_languages) languages—migrated across the [Limpopo River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limpopo_River) and came to dominate the earlier farmers both politically and culturally, organising into loosely-defined chiefdoms that held dynamic alliance networks.[^cite_note-205] Further south, the Dutch began colonizing the [western Cape](313646.md) in the 17th century, and Britain seized control of the [Dutch Cape Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Cape_Colony) around the turn of the 19th century.[^cite_note-206] In the late-18th century, the interplay of pre-existing trends of political centralization with the effects of international trade, environmental instability, and European colonisation set the *[Mfecane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mfecane)* ("Crushing") in motion, as various African states were founded.[^cite_note-207]

### c. 1800 – c. 1935

Main article: [Colonial Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Africa)



Comparison of Africa in 1880 and 1913, showing the "[Scramble for Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa)" by the European powers

By the turn of the 19th century, many of Africa's economic and political structures were unstable and brittle as a result of long-term effects of the slave trade.[^cite_note-\:02-208]<sup>: 5–6 </sup> In Egypt [Muhammad Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt) led a national movement to seize power from Ottoman suzerainty, instituting sweeping reforms and greatly strengthening Egyptian power by militaristic expansion. Meanwhile, in West Africa the [Fula *jihad*s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fula_jihads), which had been gaining momentum throughout the 18th century, culminated in the establishment of the expansive [Sokoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_Caliphate) and [Hamdullahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate_of_Hamdullahi) caliphates.[^cite_note-\:02-208]<sup>: 18–20 </sup> In the Horn, Ethiopia underwent reforms, seeking to revive the imperial state, something that was later achieved by [Menelik II's conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menelik_II's_conquests).[^cite_note-\:112-209] In Southern Africa the *[Mfecane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mfecane)* propelled large long-distance migrations that caused widespread disruption across the vast region, while several [Boer republics](364762.md) were founded. European campaigns to abolish the Atlantic slave trade led to supply refocusing on internal markets and saw the rapid growth of the [Indian Ocean slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_slave_trade).[^cite_note-\:02-208]<sup>: 5 </sup> Across the continent many rulers attempted reforms and indigenous forms of 'modernisation', however these efforts were consistently undermined by European traders and missionaries as the tide of European encroachment rose.[^cite_note-\:1-210]<sup>: 774 </sup>

For 400 years, European nations had mainly limited their involvement to trading stations on the African coast, with few daring to venture inland. The [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Industrial_Revolution) in Europe produced several technological innovations which assisted them in overcoming this 400-year pattern. One was the development of [repeating rifles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repeating_rifle), which were easier and quicker to load than [muskets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musket). [Artillery](2508.md) was being used increasingly. In 1885, [Hiram Maxim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiram_Maxim) developed the [maxim gun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxim_gun), the model of the modern-day [machine gun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_gun). European states kept these weapons largely among themselves by refusing to sell these weapons to African leaders.[^cite_note-Collins-2007-211]<sup>: 268–269 </sup> African germs took numerous European lives and deterred [permanent settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler_colonialism). Diseases such as [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever), [sleeping sickness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_trypanosomiasis), [yaws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaws), and [leprosy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leprosy) made Africa a very inhospitable place for Europeans. The deadliest disease was [malaria](20423.md), endemic throughout [Tropical Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrotropical_realm). In 1854, the discovery of [quinine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinine) and other medical innovations helped to make conquest and colonisation in Africa possible.[^cite_note-Collins-2007-211]<sup>: 269 </sup>



The [Battle of Isandlwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Isandlwana) (1879), a decisive Zulu victory over a British invasion force during the [Anglo-Zulu War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Zulu_War).

There were strong motives for the conquest of Africa. [Raw materials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resources_of_Africa) were needed for European factories, and free trade was championed to prevent Africans from regulating their economies. Prestige and imperial rivalries were at play. Acquiring African colonies would show rivals that a nation was powerful and significant. These contextual factors forged the [Scramble for Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa);[^cite_note-Collins-2007-211]<sup>: 265 </sup>[^cite_note-\:1-210]<sup>: 788 </sup> during the late-19th and early-20th centuries the [European powers partitioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Conference) and conquered almost all of Africa despite fierce resistance (only Ethiopia and [Liberia](17791.md) remained independent). Traditional leaders were incorporated into the colonial regimes as a form of indirect rule to extract human and natural resources and curb organized resistance.[^cite_note-212] Colonial borders were drawn unilaterally by the Europeans, often cutting across bonds of kinship, language, culture, and established routes, and sometimes incorporating groups who previously had little in common. The threat to trade routes was mitigated by poor policing and African entrepreneurs (viewed as smugglers) who exploited the differing tax and legal schemes.[^cite_note-213] The first few decades of European colonialism saw intense violence, disorder, and atrocities, such as that of the [Congo Free State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atrocities_in_the_Congo_Free_State), [German South West Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herero_and_Nama_genocide), and [French Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_genocide), with the late colonial period relatively peaceful in comparison. Colonial rule had [wide-reaching effects on African societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analysis_of_European_colonialism_and_colonization), such as the [spread of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_and_colonialism) as part of the '[civilising mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilising_mission)'. African culture was strongly denigrated and persecuted, as pervasive racial discrimination deepened the scars of humiliation.[^cite_note-214]<sup>: 784–5, 803–4 </sup>

### Independence struggles



European colonial presence in Africa as of 1939

Imperial rule by Europeans continued until after the conclusion of [World War II](32927.md), when almost all remaining colonial territories gradually obtained formal independence. [Independence movements in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation_of_Africa) gained momentum following World War II, which left the major European powers weakened. In 1951, Libya, a former Italian colony, gained independence. In 1956, Tunisia and Morocco won their independence from France.[^cite_note-215] Ghana followed suit in 1957),[^cite_note-216] becoming the first of the sub-Saharan colonies to be granted independence. Over the next decade, waves of decolonisation took place across the continent, culminating in the 1960 [Year of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Year_of_Africa) and the establishment of the [Organisation of African Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_African_Unity) in 1963.[^cite_note-Hargreaves_1996-36]

Portugal's overseas presence in sub-Saharan Africa (most notably in [Angola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Angola), Cape Verde, [Mozambique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Mozambique), [Guinea-Bissau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Guinea), and São Tomé and Príncipe) lasted from the 16th century to 1975, after the [Estado Novo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estado_Novo_\(Portugal\)) regime was overthrown in [a military coup in Lisbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnation_Revolution). [Rhodesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodesia) [unilaterally declared independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodesia's_Unilateral_Declaration_of_Independence) from the United Kingdom in 1965, under the white minority government of [Ian Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Smith) but was not internationally recognised as an independent state (as [Zimbabwe](34399.md)) until 1980, when black nationalists gained power after a [bitter guerrilla war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodesian_Bush_War). Although South Africa was one of the first African countries to gain independence, the state remained under the control of the country's white minority, initially through qualified voting rights and from 1956 by a system of [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation) known as [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid), until 1994.

### Post-colonial Africa

Main article: [Postcolonial Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postcolonial_Africa)

See also: [Decolonisation of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation_of_Africa), [Neocolonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neocolonialism), [Status of forces agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_of_forces_agreement), and [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement)



1988 Soviet commemorative stamp, captioned "Fighter for the freedom of South Africa [Nelson Mandela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela)" in Russian

As of 2025, Africa contains 54 sovereign countries.[^cite_note-217] Since independence, African states have frequently been hampered by instability, corruption, violence, and authoritarianism. The vast majority of African states are republics that operate under some form of the [presidential system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_system) of rule. However, few of them have been able to sustain democratic governments on a permanent basis—per the criteria laid out by Lührmann et al. (2018), only [Botswana](3464.md) and [Mauritius](19201.md) have been consistently democratic for the entirety of their post-colonial history. Most African countries have experienced several coups or periods of [military dictatorship](146728.md). Between 1990 and 2018, though, the continent as a whole has trended towards more democratic governance.[^cite_note-218]

Upon independence an overwhelming majority of Africans lived in extreme poverty. The continent suffered from the lack of infrastructural or industrial development under colonial rule, along with political instability. With limited financial resources or access to global markets, relatively stable countries such as [Kenya](188171.md) still experienced only very slow economic development. Only a handful of countries succeeded in obtaining rapid economic growth prior to 1990. Exceptions include Libya and Equatorial Guinea, both of which possess large oil reserves.

Instability after decolonisation resulted primarily from [marginalisation of ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_racism), and corruption. In pursuit of personal political gain, many leaders deliberately promoted ethnic conflicts, some of which had originated during the colonial period, such as from the grouping of multiple unrelated ethnic groups into a single colony, the splitting of a distinct ethnic group between multiple colonies, or existing conflicts being exacerbated by colonial rule (for instance, the preferential treatment given to ethnic [Hutus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutu) over [Tutsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutsi) in Rwanda during German and Belgian rule).

Faced with increasingly frequent and severe violence, military rule was widely accepted by the population of many countries as means to maintain order, and during the 1970s and 1980s a majority of African countries were controlled by military dictatorships. Territorial disputes between nations and rebellions by groups seeking independence were also common in independent African states. The most devastating of these was the [Nigerian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Civil_War), fought between government forces and an [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people) [separatist republic](44431.md), which resulted in a famine that killed 1–2 million people. Two civil wars in Sudan, [the first](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Sudanese_Civil_War) lasting from 1955 to 1972 and [the second](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Sudanese_Civil_War) from 1983 to 2005, collectively killed around 3 million. Both were fought primarily on ethnic and religious lines.

[Cold War](325329.md) conflicts between the United States and the Soviet Union also contributed to instability. Both the Soviet Union and the United States offered considerable incentives to political and military leaders who aligned themselves with the superpowers' foreign policy. As an example, during the [Angolan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angolan_Civil_War), the Soviet and Cuban aligned [MPLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MPLA) and the American aligned [UNITA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNITA) received the vast majority of their military and political support from these countries. Many African countries became highly dependent on foreign aid. The sudden loss of both Soviet and American aid at the end of the Cold War and [fall of the USSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_USSR) resulted in severe economic and political turmoil in the countries most dependent on foreign support.

There was a [major famine in Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983%E2%80%931985_famine_in_Ethiopia) between 1983 and 1985, killing up to 1.2 million people, which most historians attribute primarily to the forced relocation of farmworkers and seizure of grain by the communist [Derg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derg) government, further exacerbated by the [Ethiopian Civil War](6572888.md).[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] In 1994 a [genocide in Rwanda](676659.md) resulted in up to 800,000 deaths, added to [a severe refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes_refugee_crisis) and fuelled the rise of militia groups in neighbouring countries. This contributed to the outbreak of the [first](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Congo_War) and [second](877492.md) Congo Wars, which were the most devastating military conflicts in modern Africa, with up to 5.5 million deaths,[^cite_note-223] making it by far the deadliest conflict in modern African history and one of the [costliest wars in human history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_by_death_toll).[^cite_note-224] In 2025 it is estimated 20% of Africans face hunger in 2025, compared to 6% of Asia, 5% in Latin America and the Caribbean, 8% in Oceania, and less than 2.5% in North America and Europe.[^cite_note-225]

- An animated map shows the order of independence of African nations, 1950–2011.

  An animated map shows the order of [independence of African nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation_of_Africa), 1950–2011.

- Africa's wars and conflicts, 1980–96  Major wars/conflicts (>100,000 casualties)  Minor wars/conflicts  Other conflicts

  Africa's wars and conflicts, 1980–96\


  &#x20; Major wars/conflicts (>100,000 casualties)

  &#x20; Minor wars/conflicts

  &#x20; Other conflicts

- Political map of Africa in 2021

  Political map of Africa in 2021

Various conflicts between various insurgent groups and governments continue. From 2003-2020 a [conflict in Darfur](5043637.md) resulted in around 300,000 deaths. The [Boko Haram insurgency](27868253.md) in Sub-Saharan Africa has killed around 350,000 people since 2009. The [Tigray war](65760352.md) from 2020 to 2022 killed an estimated 300,000–500,000 people, primarily due to [famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine_in_the_Tigray_War). Overall, violence across Africa has greatly declined in the 21st century, with the end of civil wars in Angola, [Sierra Leone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_Leone_Civil_War), and [Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_Civil_War) in 2002, [Liberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Liberian_Civil_War) in 2003, and [Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Sudanese_Civil_War) and [Burundi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burundian_Civil_War) in 2005. The Second Congo War, which involved 9 countries and several insurgent groups, ended in 2003. This decline in violence coincided with many countries abandoning communist-style command economies and opening up for market reforms, which over the course of the 1990s and 2000s promoted the establishment of permanent, peaceful trade between neighbouring countries.&#x20;

Improved stability and economic reforms have led to a great increase in foreign investment into many nations, mainly from China,[^cite_note-Africa-226] which further spurred economic growth.
