Between 2000 and 2014, annual GDP growth in sub-Saharan Africa averaged 5%, doubling its total GDP from \$811 billion to \$1.63 trillion (constant 2015 [USD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USD)).[^cite_note-227] North Africa experienced comparable growth rates.[^cite_note-228] A significant part of this growth can also be attributed to the facilitated diffusion of information technologies and specifically the mobile telephone.[^cite_note-229] While several individual countries have maintained high growth rates, since 2014 overall growth has considerably slowed, primarily as a result of falling commodity prices, continued lack of industrialisation, and epidemics such as [Ebola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_African_Ebola_virus_epidemic) and [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Africa).[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Africa)



Topography of Africa

Africa is the largest of the three great southward projections from the largest [landmass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmass) of the Earth. Separated from Europe by the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md), it is joined to Asia at its northeast extremity by the [Isthmus of Suez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isthmus_of_Suez) (transected by the [Suez Canal](29323.md)), 163 km (101 mi) wide.[^cite_note-232] Geopolitically, Egypt's [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula) east of the Suez Canal is often considered part of Africa as well.[^cite_note-233]



The size of Africa compared to other continents



Africa seen from the [Moon](19331.md) on *[The Blue Marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blue_Marble)* photograph, 1972

The coastline is 26,000 km (16,000 mi) long, and the absence of deep indentations of the shore is illustrated by the fact that Europe, which covers only 10,400,000 km<sup>2</sup> (4,000,000 sq mi) – about a third of the surface of Africa – has a coastline of 32,000 km (20,000 mi).[^cite_note-MW-234] From the most northerly point, [Ras ben Sakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ras_ben_Sakka) in Tunisia (37°21' N), to the most southerly point, [Cape Agulhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Agulhas) in South Africa (34°51'15" S), is a distance of approximately 8,000 km (5,000 mi).[^cite_note-235] [Cape Verde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap-Vert), 17°33'22" W, the westernmost point, is a distance of approximately 7,400 km (4,600 mi) to [Ras Hafun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ras_Hafun), 51°27'52" E, the most easterly projection that neighbours [Cape Guardafui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Guardafui), the tip of the Horn of Africa.[^cite_note-MW-234]

The largest country is Algeria and smallest is [Seychelles](27288.md), an [archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelago) off the east coast.[^cite_note-Hoare-236] The smallest nation on the continental mainland is [The Gambia](65656.md).

### African plate

This section is an excerpt from [African plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_plate).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=African_plate\&action=edit)]

The [African plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_plate), also known as the Nubian plate, is a [major tectonic plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tectonic_plates#Major_plates) that includes most of the [continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continent) of Africa (except for its [easternmost part](260987.md)) and the adjacent oceanic crust to the west and south. It also includes a narrow strip of [Western Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia) along the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md), including much of [Israel](9282173.md), [Palestine](241405.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md). It is bounded by the [North American plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_plate) and [South American plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_American_plate) to the west (separated by the [Mid-Atlantic Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-Atlantic_Ridge)); the [Arabian plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_plate) and [Somali plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somali_plate) to the east; the [Eurasian plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_plate), [Aegean Sea plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea_plate) and [Anatolian plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_plate) to the north; and the [Antarctic plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctic_plate) to the south.

Between [60](https://geoltime.github.io/?Ma=60) million years ago and [10](https://geoltime.github.io/?Ma=10) million years ago, the [Somali plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somali_plate) began [rifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rift_\(geology\)) from the African plate along the [East African Rift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_African_Rift).[^cite_note-237] Since the continent of Africa consists of crust from both the African and the Somali plates, some literature refers to the African plate as the *Nubian plate* to distinguish it from the continent as a whole.[^cite_note-238]



Today, the African Plate is moving over Earth's surface at a speed of 0.292° ± 0.007° per million years, relative to the "average" Earth (NNR-MORVEL56).

### Climate

The [climate of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Africa) ranges from [tropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_climate) to [subarctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subarctic_climate) on its highest peaks. Its northern half is primarily [desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert) or [arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arid), while its central and southern areas contain both [savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savanna) plains and dense [jungle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jungle) ([rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainforest)) regions. In between, there is a convergence, where vegetation patterns such as [sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel) and [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe) dominate. Africa is the hottest continent on Earth, and 60% of the land surface consists of drylands and deserts.[^cite_note-environmentalatlas-239] The record for the highest-ever recorded temperature, in [Libya](17633.md) in 1922 (58 °C (136 °F)), was discredited in 2013.[^cite_note-newRecord-240][^cite_note-241]

### Climate change

This section is an excerpt from [Climate change in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Africa).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Climate_change_in_Africa\&action=edit)]



Graph showing [temperature change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrumental_temperature_record) in Africa between 1901 and 2021, with red colour being warmer and blue being colder than average (The average temperature during 1971–2000 is taken as the reference point for these changes.)

[Climate change in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Africa) is a serious threat because the continent is one of the most [vulnerable](66204363.md) regions to the [effects of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change), despite being responsible for less than 4% of global greenhouse emissions.[^cite_note-242] Climate change is causing increasingly erratic rainfall patterns, more frequent [extreme weather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_weather) events such as [droughts](37335.md), [floods](50482.md), and rising [sea-surface temperatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_surface_temperature) across the continent. These changes threaten [food](216361.md) and [water security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_security), [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity), [public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health), and [economic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_development).[^cite_note-Climate_change_in_Africa_\:02-243][^cite_note-244] Africa is currently warming faster than the rest of the world on average.[^cite_note-245]

Climate change intensifies existing socioeconomic vulnerabilities. Large segments of the African population depend on climate-sensitive livelihoods such as agriculture (55 - 62% of the workforce in sub-Saharan Africa)[^cite_note-246] and already live in poverty, heightening their exposure to shocks. Health outcomes worsen as [heat stress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperthermia), [vector-borne diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disease_vector) (such as [malaria](20423.md) and [dengue](39669.md)), and [malnutrition](258979.md) become more prevalent. Over half (56%) of the over 2,000 recorded public health incidents in Africa between 2001 and 2021 were connected to climate change.[^cite_note-247] [Resource scarcity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_depletion) contributes to [displacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_migration) and conflict, particularly in fragile regions. [Urban areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area), often characterized by informal settlements, face heightened risks from flooding and extreme heat.[^cite_note-Climate_change_in_Africa_\:02-243]



Lake kariba

### Ecology and biodiversity

See also: [Afrotropical realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrotropical_realm) and [Palearctic realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palearctic_realm)



The main biomes in Africa

Africa has over 3,000 [protected areas](100406.md), with 198 marine protected areas, 50 biosphere reserves, and 80 wetlands reserves. Significant habitat destruction, increases in human population, and poaching are reducing the biological diversity and [arable land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land). Human encroachment, civil unrest and the introduction of non-native species threaten biodiversity. This has been exacerbated by administrative problems, inadequate personnel and funding problems.[^cite_note-environmentalatlas-239]

[Deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation) is affecting Africa at twice the world rate, according to the [United Nations Environment Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Environment_Programme).[^cite_note-248] The deforestation rate of 4.08 million hectares in 2000–2015 declined to 3.45 million ha per year in 2015–2025.[^cite_note-249] According to the University of Pennsylvania African Studies Center, 31% of pasture lands and 19% of forests and woodlands are classified as degraded, and the deforestation rate is twice the average for the rest of the world.[^cite_note-environmentalatlas-239] Some sources claim that approximately 90% of the original, virgin forests in West Africa have been destroyed.[^cite_note-250] Over 90% of [Madagascar](18964.md)'s original forests have been destroyed since the arrival of humans 2,000 years ago.[^cite_note-251] About 65% of agricultural land suffers from [soil degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_retrogression_and_degradation).[^cite_note-252]

### Fauna

Main article: [Fauna of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Africa)



The [savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savanna) of [Ngorongoro Conservation Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngorongoro_Conservation_Area) in [Tanzania](30118.md)

Africa boasts perhaps the world's largest combination of density and "range of freedom" of wild animal populations and diversity, with wild populations of large carnivores (such as [lions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion), [hyenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyena), and [cheetahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheetah)) and herbivores (such as [buffalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_buffalo), [elephants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elephant), [camels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camel), and [giraffes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giraffe)) ranging freely on primarily open non-private plains. It is also home to a variety of "jungle" animals including snakes and primates and aquatic life such as crocodiles and amphibians. Africa has the largest number of [megafauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megafauna) species, as it was least affected by the [extinction of the Pleistocene megafauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Pleistocene_extinctions).

### Environmental issues

This section is an excerpt from [Environmental issues in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Africa).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Environmental_issues_in_Africa\&action=edit)]

[African environmental problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Africa) are problems caused by the direct and indirect [human impacts on the natural environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_impact_on_the_environment) and affect humans and nearly all forms of life in Africa. Issues include [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_Africa), [soil degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_retrogression_and_degradation), [air pollution](10934212.md), water pollution, [coastal erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_erosion), garbage pollution, [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Africa), [Oil spills](158705.md), [Biodiversity loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_loss), and [water scarcity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_scarcity_in_Africa) (resulting in problems with access to safe [water supply and sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Sub-Saharan_Africa)).[^cite_note-253] These issues result in [environmental conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_conflict) and are connected to broader social struggles for [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) and [sovereignty](28791.md).[^cite_note-254] The scarcity of climate adaptation techniques in Africa makes it the least resilient continent to climate change.

## Politics

See also: [Elections in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Africa), [Democracy in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_Africa), and [List of political parties in Africa by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Africa_by_country)

### African Union



Regions of the [African Union](10634933.md):

&#x20; Northern

&#x20; Western

&#x20; Southern

&#x20; Eastern

&#x20; Central



[Democracy in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_Africa) according to 2026 [V-Dem Electoral Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices)

[^cite_note-j496-255]

The [African Union](10634933.md) (AU) is a [continental union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_union) consisting of [55 member states](30486666.md). The union was formed with [Addis Ababa](52593.md) as its headquarters in 2001[^cite_note-African_Union_2002-256] as a successor to the [Organisation of African Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_African_Unity). The [Pan-African Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-African_Parliament) (PAP) is located in [Midrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midrand), South Africa.

The African Union, not to be confused with the [AU Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Union_Commission), is formed by the [Constitutive Act of the African Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutive_Act_of_the_African_Union), which aims to transform the [African Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Economic_Community), a federated commonwealth, into a state under established international conventions. The [Assembly of the African Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_of_the_African_Union) consists of legislative, judicial and executive organs. It is led by the African Union president and head of state, who is also the president of PAP. The president is elected by PAP. The powers and authority of the president of the Assembly derive from the Constitutive Act and the Protocol of the Pan-African Parliament, as well as the inheritance of presidential authority stipulated by African treaties and by international treaties. The government consists of all-union, regional, state, and municipal authorities, as well as hundreds of institutions, that together manage the day-to-day affairs of the institution.

[Extensive human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Africa) still occur in several parts of Africa, often under the oversight of the state. Most of such violations occur for political reasons, often as a side effect of civil war. Countries where major human rights violations have been reported in recent times include the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Sierra Leone, Liberia, Sudan, Zimbabwe, and Ivory Coast.

### Boundary conflicts

Further information: [The Berlin Conference of 1884–1885](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Berlin_Conference_of_1884%E2%80%931885) and [List of conflicts in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conflicts_in_Africa)

This section is an excerpt from [Military history of Africa § Post-colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Africa#Post-colonial).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Military_history_of_Africa\&action=edit)]

African nations have made great efforts to respect international borders as inviolate for a long time. For example, the [Organization of African Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_African_Unity) (OAU), which was established in 1963 and replaced by the [African Union](10634933.md) in 2002, set the respect for the territorial integrity of each country as one of its principles in OAU Charter.[^cite_note-257] Indeed, compared with the formation of European countries, there have been fewer international conflicts in Africa for changing the borders, which has influenced country formation there and has enabled some countries to survive that might have been defeated and absorbed by others.[^cite_note-Military_history_of_Africa_Herbst-258] Yet international conflicts have played out by support for proxy armies or rebel movements. Many states have experienced civil wars: including Rwanda, Sudan, Angola, Sierra Leone, Congo, Liberia, Ethiopia and Somalia.[^cite_note-259] Wars of national liberation also often took up a Marxist, Marxist–Leninist, or Maoist character in ideological terms, where the independence of African nations was raised in Marxian terms. Early examples included the [Union of the Peoples of Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Peoples_of_Cameroon) armed movement against French colonialism, a Marxist–Leninist insurgency, followed by similar movements in Congo (the [Simba Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simba_Rebellion)). [Che Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara) participated in the latter, and opined in 1967 that Africa was not ready for revolution. Despite this, in [Zanzibar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zanzibar) before and after unification with Tanzania, forms of socialism took hold. In Sudan under [Gaafar Nimeiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaafar_Nimeiry), in Somalia under [Siad Barre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siad_Barre), in Ethiopia under the Dergue and [Mengistu Haile Mariam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mengistu_Haile_Mariam), in Congo Brazzaville under various administration from the 1960s onwards including that of [Denis Sassou-Nguesso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denis_Sassou-Nguesso), in Benin under [Mathieu Kerekou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathieu_Kerekou), in the Seychelles under [France-Albert René](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France-Albert_Ren%C3%A9), in Angola, Mozambique and Guinea-Bissau before Portugal's 1974 Carnation Revolution, Marxism–Leninism was fashionable. Even after 1990, there are some examples of Military Marxism, such as a militia established by [Ernest Wamba-dia-Wamba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Wamba-dia-Wamba) in Eastern Congo in the 1990s.[^cite_note-260]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Africa](1055517.md)

See also: [Economy of the African Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_African_Union), [List of African countries by GDP (nominal)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)), and [List of African countries by GDP (PPP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\))



Map of the [African Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Economic_Community)&#x20;

&#x20; [CEN-SAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEN-SAD)

&#x20; [COMESA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COMESA)

&#x20; [EAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_African_Community)

&#x20; [ECCAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ECCAS)

&#x20; [ECOWAS](36848.md)

&#x20; [IGAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IGAD)

&#x20; [SADC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_African_Development_Community)

&#x20; [UMA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Maghreb_Union)



African countries by [GDP](12594.md) (PPP) per capita in 2023

Although it has abundant natural resources, Africa remains the world's poorest and [least-developed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index) continent (other than Antarctica), the result of a variety of causes that may include [corrupt governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) that have often committed serious human rights violations, failed central planning, high levels of illiteracy, low self-esteem, lack of access to foreign capital, legacies of colonialism, the slave trade, the Cold War, and frequent tribal and military conflict (ranging from guerrilla warfare to genocide).[^cite_note-270] The continent's total nominal GDP remains behind that of several individual countries, including the United States, China, Japan, Germany, the United Kingdom, India and France. According to the United Nations' Human Development Report in 2003, the bottom 24 ranked nations (151st to 175th) were all African.[^cite_note-271]

[Poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Africa), illiteracy, malnutrition, inadequate [water supply and sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WASH), and poor health affect a large proportion of the people. A 2008 [World Bank](36752.md)[^cite_note-272] estimates 81% of the sub-Saharan African population were living on less than \$2.50 (PPP) per day in 2005, compared with 86% for India.[^cite_note-273] Sub-Saharan Africa is the least successful region of the world in reducing poverty; some 50% of the population living in poverty in 1981 (200 million people), a figure that rose to 58% in 1996 before dropping to 50% in 2005 (380 million people). The average poor person in sub-Saharan Africa is estimated to live on only 70 cents per day and was poorer in 2003 than in 1973,[^cite_note-274] indicating increasing poverty in some areas. Some of it is attributed to unsuccessful economic liberalisation programmes spearheaded by foreign companies and governments, but other studies cite bad domestic government policies more than external factors.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276]

The last debt crisis in 2005 was resolved with help from the [heavily indebted poor countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavily_indebted_poor_countries) scheme, which resulted in some positive and negative effects on the economy in Africa. About 10 years after the 2005 debt crisis in sub-Saharan Africa was resolved, Zambia fell back into debt. A small reason was due to the fall in copper prices in 2011, but the bigger reason was that a large amount of the money Zambia borrowed was wasted or pocketed by the elite.[^cite_note-277]

From 1995 to 2005, Africa's rate of economic growth increased, averaging 5% in 2005. Some countries experienced still higher growth rates, notably Angola, Sudan and Equatorial Guinea, all of which had increased oil production.

The continent is believed to hold 90% of the world's [cobalt](24580536.md), 90% of its [platinum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platinum), 50% of its gold, 98% of its [chromium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromium), 70% of its [tantalite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantalite),[^cite_note-279] 64% of its [manganese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manganese) and one-third of its [uranium](31743.md).[^cite_note-280] The DRC has 70% of the world's [coltan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coltan), a mineral used in the production of [tantalum capacitors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantalum_capacitor) for electronic devices such as cell phones. The DRC also has more than 30% of the world's diamond reserves.[^cite_note-281] Guinea is the world's largest exporter of [bauxite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauxite).[^cite_note-282] As the growth in Africa has been driven mainly by services and not manufacturing or agriculture, it has been growth without jobs and without reduction in poverty levels. In fact, the [food security crisis of 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%932008_world_food_price_crisis) (which took place following the [global financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis)) pushed 100 million people into food insecurity.[^cite_note-283] In 2024 nearly 307 million people were undernourished (46% of the global total), an increase of 10 million people since 2023 and 34 million from 2022.[^cite_note-284]

China has built increasingly stronger ties with African nations and is Africa's largest trading partner. In 2007, Chinese companies invested a total of US\$1 billion in Africa.[^cite_note-Africa-226]

A [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University) study led by professor [Calestous Juma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calestous_Juma) shows that Africa could feed itself by making the transition from importer to self-sufficiency. "African agriculture is at the crossroads; we have come to the end of a century of policies that favoured Africa's export of raw materials and importation of food. Africa is starting to focus on agricultural innovation as its new engine for regional trade and prosperity."[^cite_note-285]

### Energy

This section is an excerpt from [Energy in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Africa).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Energy_in_Africa\&action=edit)]









Cooking with biogas in Tanzania; Geothermal energy infrastructure in Kenya; Coal power station in South Africa; Solar-powered lamp-light in Zambia.

[Energy in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Africa) is characterised by low [per-capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita) consumption and limited access to modern energy services, reflecting both infrastructural and socio-economic challenges across the continent. As of 2023, Africa accounted for just 5.5% of global total final energy consumption, with per-capita energy use more than three times lower than the global average. The [energy mix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_mix) is dominated by [biofuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofuel) and waste, which make up 43% of total final consumption, primarily for household cooking and heating, followed by oil products at 33%, electricity at 11%, natural gas at 9%, and coal at 4%.

[Electricity access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrification) remains a major challenge, particularly in Sub-Saharan Africa, where nearly 450 million rural residents lack reliable electricity,[^cite_note-286] and grid coverage is often inconsistent.[^cite_note-287] [Clean cooking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_poverty_and_cooking) solutions are similarly underdeveloped, leaving over 900 million people reliant on polluting fuels such as firewood, charcoal, and agricultural residues, which disproportionately affect women and children. Transport energy is dominated by oil products, reflecting low motorisation rates and limited adoption of electric mobility, although countries such as Ethiopia, Morocco, and Egypt have begun adopting policies to reduce fossil fuel dependency.

Africa possesses some of the world's most abundant energy resources, including [oil](4207510.md), [natural gas](22131.md), [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal), [hydropower](14073.md), [solar](13690575.md), [wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power), [geothermal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geothermal_energy), and [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass), much of which remains underutilised. While [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel) such as coal, oil, and natural gas continue to dominate energy production and electricity generation in Africa, [renewable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_Africa) are increasingly expanding across the continent. Hydropower, solar, wind, geothermal, and modern biomass are growing in capacity, though their deployment remains uneven and limited compared to the continent's vast potential. Hydropower is the largest non-combustible renewable source, while solar energy offers immense potential due to high irradiation levels, particularly in the [Sahara](325363.md) and [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel).&#x20;

There are regional differences: [North Africa](21714.md) is dominated by oil and gas production, [Southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa) by coal and hydropower, [East Africa](260987.md) by hydropower and geothermal, and [West](67393.md) and [Central Africa](539606.md) face major electrification gaps despite hydroelectric potential.

### Infrastructure

#### Water resources

See also: [Water scarcity in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_scarcity_in_Africa) and [Water supply and sanitation in sub-Saharan Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_sub-Saharan_Africa)

Water development and management are complex due to the multiplicity of trans-boundary water resources (rivers, lakes and aquifers).[^cite_note-Water_2016-288] Around 75% of sub-Saharan Africa falls within 53 international river basin catchments that traverse multiple borders.[^cite_note-Coop_Water-289][^cite_note-Water_2016-288] This particular constraint can also be converted into an opportunity if the potential for trans-boundary cooperation is harnessed in the development of the area's water resources.[^cite_note-Water_2016-288] A multi-sectoral analysis of the [Zambezi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambezi) River, for example, shows that [riparian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riparian_zone) cooperation could lead to a 23% increase in firm energy production without any additional investments.[^cite_note-Coop_Water-289][^cite_note-Water_2016-288] Several institutional and legal frameworks for transboundary cooperation exist, such as the Zambezi River Authority, the [Southern African Development Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_African_Development_Community) protocol, [Volta River Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volta_River_Authority) and the Nile Basin Commission.[^cite_note-Water_2016-288] However, additional efforts are required to further develop political will as well as the financial capacities and institutional frameworks needed for multilateral cooperative actions and optimal solutions for all riparians.[^cite_note-Water_2016-288]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Africa), [Genetic history of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_Africa), and [Indigenous peoples of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_Africa)

See also: [List of African countries by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_population) and [List of African countries by life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_life_expectancy)

[Proportion of total African population by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_population)

1. Nigeria (15.4%)

2. Ethiopia (8.37%)

3. Egypt (7.65%)

4. Democratic Republic of the Congo (6.57%)

5. Tanzania (4.55%)

6. South Africa (4.47%)

7. Kenya (3.88%)

8. Uganda (3.38%)

9. Algeria (3.36%)

10. Other (42.4%)

Africa is considered by anthropologists to be the most genetically diverse continent as a result of being the longest inhabited.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] The population has rapidly increased over the last 40 years and is consequently relatively young. In some states, more than half the population is under 25 years of age.[^cite_note-293] The population increased from 229 million in 1950 to 630 million in 1990.[^cite_note-294] As of 2021, the population is estimated at 1,394 million.[^cite_note-UN_WPP-1][^cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-2] Africa's total population surpassing other continents is fairly recent; the population surpassed Europe in the 1990s, while the Americas was overtaken sometime around 2000.[^cite_note-295] This increase in number of babies born in Africa compared to the rest of the world is expected to reach approximately 37% in the year 2050; while in 1990 sub-Saharan Africa accounted for only 16% of the world's births.[^cite_note-296]

The [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) (children per woman) for sub-Saharan Africa is 4.7 as of 2018, the highest in the world.[^cite_note-297] All countries in sub-Saharan Africa had rates above replacement level in 2019 and accounted for 27.1% of global live births.[^cite_note-298] In 2021, sub-Saharan Africa accounted for 29% of global births.[^cite_note-299]

Speakers of [Bantu languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_languages) (part of the [Niger–Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger%E2%80%93Congo_languages) family) are the majority in southern, central and southeast Africa. The Bantu-speaking peoples from the Sahel progressively expanded over most of sub-Saharan Africa.[^cite_note-300] But there are also several [Nilotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nilotic_languages) groups in South Sudan and East Africa, the mixed [Swahili people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swahili_people) on the [Swahili coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swahili_coast), and a few remaining indigenous [Khoisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoisan) and Pygmy peoples in Southern and Central Africa, respectively. Bantu speakers predominate in Gabon and Equatorial Guinea and are found in parts of southern Cameroon. In the [Kalahari Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalahari_Desert) the distinct people known as the Bushmen (also "[San](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_peoples)", closely related to but distinct from [Khoekhoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoekhoe)) have long been present. The San are physically distinct from other Africans and are the indigenous people of southern Africa. Pygmies are the pre-Bantu indigenous peoples of Central Africa.[^cite_note-301]

The peoples of West Africa primarily speak Niger–Congo languages, belonging mostly to its non-Bantu branches, though some [Nilo-Saharan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nilo-Saharan_languages) and [Afroasiatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afroasiatic_languages) speaking groups are also found. The Niger–Congo-speaking [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_language), [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_language), [Fulani](102383.md), [Akan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twi-Fante_language), and [Wolof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolof_people) ethnic groups are the largest and most influential. In the central Sahara, [Mandinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandinka_people) or [Mande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mande_languages) groups are most significant. [Chadic-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chadic_languages) groups, including the [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_language), are found in more northerly parts of the region nearest to the Sahara. Nilo-Saharan communities such as the [Songhai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songhai_people), [Kanuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanuri_people), and [Zarma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarma_people) are found in eastern West Africa.

| 0.800–0.849   0.750–0.799   0.700–0.749   0.650–0.699   0.600–0.649   0.550–0.599    0.500–0.549   0.450–0.499   0.400–0.449   ≤ 0.399   No data |

The peoples of North Africa consist of three main indigenous groups: [Berbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berbers) in the northwest, [Egyptians](31912046.md) in the northeast, and Nilo-Saharan-speaking peoples in the east. The Arabs who arrived in the 7th century introduced the Arabic language and Islam to North Africa. The Semitic [Phoenicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicia) (who founded [Carthage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthage)) and [Hyksos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyksos), the Indo-Iranian [Alans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alans), the Indo-European [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), Romans, and [Vandals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandals) settled in North Africa as well. Significant Berber communities remain within Morocco and Algeria in the 21st century, while, Berber speakers are also present in some regions of Tunisia and Libya.[^cite_note-302] The Berber-speaking [Tuareg](103054.md) and other often nomadic peoples are the principal inhabitants of the Saharan interior of North Africa. In Mauritania, there is a small nearly extinct Berber community in the north and Niger–Congo-speaking peoples in the south, though in both regions Arabic and Arab culture predominates. In Sudan, although Arabic and Arab culture predominate, it is mostly inhabited by groups that originally spoke Nilo-Saharan, such as the [Nubians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nubians), [Fur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_people), [Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit_people) and [Zaghawa](1467480.md), who, over the centuries, have variously intermixed with migrants from the Arabian peninsula. Small communities of Afroasiatic-speaking [Beja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beja_people) nomads are found in Egypt and Sudan.[^cite_note-303]

In the Horn of Africa, some [Ethiopian](6204551.md) and [Eritrean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eritreans) groups (like the [Amhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amhara_people) and [Tigrayans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigrayans), collectively known as [Habesha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habesha_peoples)) speak languages from the [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages) branch of the Afroasiatic language family, while the [Oromo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oromo_people) and [Somali](1571696.md) speak languages from the [Cushitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cushitic_languages) branch of Afroasiatic.

Prior to the decolonisation movements of the post-World War II era, Europeans were represented in every part of Africa.[^cite_note-304] Decolonization during the 1960s and 1970s often resulted in the mass emigration of white settlers—especially from Algeria and Morocco (1.6 million *[pieds-noirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pieds-noir)* in North Africa),[^cite_note-305] Kenya, Congo,[^cite_note-306] Rhodesia, Mozambique and Angola.[^cite_note-307] Between 1975 and 1977, over a million colonials returned to Portugal alone.[^cite_note-308] Nevertheless, [white Africans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Africans_of_European_ancestry) remain an important minority in many African states, particularly Zimbabwe, Namibia, Réunion, and South Africa.[^cite_note-309] The country with the largest white African population is South Africa.[^cite_note-310] [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_diaspora) and [British diasporas](1472509.md) represent the largest communities of European ancestry on the continent.[^cite_note-World-311]

European colonisation brought [sizable groups of Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_diaspora), particularly [South Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_diaspora), to British colonies. Large [Indian communities](26998691.md) are found in South Africa, and smaller ones are present in Kenya, Tanzania, and some other southern and southeast African countries. The large [Indian community in Uganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indians_in_Uganda) was [expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Asians_from_Uganda) by [Idi Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idi_Amin) in 1972, though many have since returned. The islands in the Indian Ocean are populated primarily by people of Asian origin, often mixed with Africans and Europeans. The [Malagasy peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malagasy_peoples) of Madagascar are an [Austronesian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_peoples), but those along the coast are generally mixed with Bantu, Arab, Indian and European origins. Malay and Indian ancestries are components in [Cape Coloureds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Coloureds) (people with origins in two or more races and continents). During the 20th century, small but economically important communities of [Lebanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Lebanon#Diaspora)[^cite_note-Africa-226] have developed in West and East Africa.[^cite_note-312]

### Genetic history

Main articles: [Genetic history of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_Africa), [Genetic history of North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_North_Africa), [Genetic history of East Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_East_Africa), [Genetic history of West Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_West_Africa), [Genetic history of Central Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_Central_Africa), [Genetic history of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_South_Africa), and [Genetic history of the African diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_the_African_diaspora)



[San people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_people) lived in southern Africa before the Bantu expansion

Africa has the greatest human genetic diversity of any continent. Ancient DNA and genomic studies reveal deep population structure, with substantial differentiation among ancestral populations in southern, eastern, central, western, and northern Africa, alongside repeated [migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-modern_human_migration) and admixture. Some genetic models also infer [admixture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interbreeding_between_archaic_and_modern_humans) with deeply divergent, now-extinct ["ghost" populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghost_population) of [archaic humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo) in Africa, whose fossil or genomic identity remains unknown. Later demographic expansions, including the [spread of Bantu-speaking populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_expansion), substantially reshaped this genetic landscape. Ancient genomes also show distinct demographic histories in North Africa, including long-term regional continuity and later gene flow from Eurasia and elsewhere in Africa.[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319]

The [Sahara](325363.md) served as a trans-regional passageway and place of dwelling for people in Africa during various [humid phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstadial)[^cite_note-Osborne-320][^cite_note-Drake-321][^cite_note-El-Shenawy-322] and periods throughout the [history of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Africa).[^cite_note-Scheele-323][^cite_note-Wippel-324] Early Holocene genetic evidence indicates contacts between populations of the Lake Chad Basin and North Africa. The mitochondrial DNA haplogroup [L3e5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_L3_\(mtDNA\)), which probably originated in the Lake Chad Basin around 10,000 years ago, occurs both there and in parts of North Africa, suggesting gene flow across the Sahara during a period of increased humidity.[^cite_note-325]



[E1b1b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E-M215) is a major Y-chromosomal haplogroup in North and East Africa. Some phylogeographic studies place the origin of major E1b1b lineages in eastern Africa.

[^cite_note-326]

Ancient DNA provides evidence for substantial genetic continuity in North Africa alongside contacts with populations from Western Asia. A whole genome from an individual buried at Nuwayrat in Middle Egypt around 2855–2570 BCE was modelled as predominantly deriving from North African Neolithic ancestry, with about 20% of his ancestry related to populations of the eastern [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent), including Mesopotamia. Later ancient Egyptians also show increased ancestry related to the Levant, while present-day Egyptians have additional ancestry from sub-Saharan Africa and other regions.[^cite_note-327] Ancient genomes from the [Green Sahara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Sahara) likewise indicate the existence of a deeply divergent North African lineage, closely related to ancestry found in 15,000-year-old [Taforalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taforalt) foragers in Morocco.[^cite_note-328]

According to a recent study, the [Arab migrations to the Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migrations_to_the_Maghreb) was mainly a demographic process that heavily implied gene flow and remodelled the genetic structure of the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb) region in North Africa, rather than a mere cultural replacement as claimed by older studies.[^cite_note-\:23-329] Another study found out that the majority of [J-M267](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_J-M267) (Eu10) chromosomes in the Maghreb are due to the recent gene flow caused by the Arab migrations to the Maghreb in the first millennium CE as both southern [Qahtanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qahtanite) and northern [Adnanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adnanites) Arabs added to the heterogenous Maghrebi ethnic melting pot. The Eu10 chromosome pool in the Maghreb is derived not only from early [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) dispersions from Western Asia but to a much greater extent from recent expansions of [Arab](2185.md) tribes from the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md).[^cite_note-evidence-330] While acknowledging the genetic impact of Arabization of Northwest Africa during the Islamic period, other authors have suggested that earlier migration processes, such as the arrival of [Neolithic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution) era famers from [Western Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia) and [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe) together with [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) and [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) input from [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) and the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) were ultimately more genetically impactful.[^cite_note-Vidal-331]

David Schoenbrun, Christopher Ehret, Steven A. Brandt and Shomarka Keita (2025) have highlighted the problematic categorisation of genetic haplogroups characterised as 'African' and '[Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian)' in North African genome studies. In reference to the van de Loosdrecht et al. 2018 study on the epipalaeolithic Taforalt remains from [Morocco](19291.md), which identified the [EM35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E-M35) (primarily [EM78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E-V68)) common in north-eastern Africa but characterised the [mtDNA (female lineage haplogroups)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA) of [U6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_U) and [M1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_M_\(mtDNA\)) as 'Eurasian', the authors questioned the classification of these maternal haplogroups despite their localised and long-established presence in ancient African populations.[^cite_note-the_oxford_handbook-332] In their view, identifying a range of African populations may still remain an issue "since the idea of 'African' still gets stereotyped or restricted. (Accepting the [southwestern Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Asian)/[Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) geographical continuity with Africa eliminates a conceptual barrier related to racio-typological thinking permitting an [Africasian construct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Asia) analogous to Eurasian.)".[^cite_note-the_oxford_handbook-332]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Africa)

See also: [Christianity in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Africa), [Islam in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Africa), and [African divination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_divination)



A map showing religious distribution in Africa

While Africans profess a wide variety of religious beliefs, the majority of the people respect African religions or parts of them. However, in formal surveys or census, most people will identify with major religions that came from outside the continent, mainly through colonisation. There are several reasons for this, the main one being the colonial idea that African religious beliefs and practices are not good enough. Religious beliefs and statistics on religious affiliation are difficult to come by since they are often a sensitive topic for governments with mixed religious populations.[^cite_note-Stanford-333][^cite_note-NYT-334] According to the *[World Book Encyclopedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Book_Encyclopedia)*, Islam and Christianity are the two largest religions. Islam is most prevalent in and is the state religion of many North African countries, such as Algeria, where 99% of the population practices Islam.[^cite_note-335] The majority of people in Southern, Southeast, and Central Africa, as well as in a sizable portion of the Horn of Africa and West Africa, identify as Christians. [Coptic Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Christians) constitute a sizable minority in Egypt, and the [Ethiopian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Orthodox_Tewahedo_Church) is the largest church in Ethiopia, with 36 million and 51 million adherents.[^cite_note-336] According to [Encyclopædia Britannica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A6dia_Britannica), 45% of the population are Christians, 40% are Muslims, and 10% follow [traditional religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_African_religions). A small number of Africans are Hindu, Buddhist, Confucianist, Baháʼí, or [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Africa). There is a minority of people [who are irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Africa).

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Africa)

See also: [Writing systems of Africa § Indigenous writing systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing_systems_of_Africa#Indigenous_writing_systems)



A simplistic view of language families spoken in Africa

By most estimates, well over a thousand languages ([UNESCO](21786641.md) has estimated around two thousand) are spoken in Africa.[^cite_note-337] Most are of African origin, though some are of European or Asian origin. Africa is the most multilingual continent in the world, and it is not rare for individuals to fluently speak multiple African languages as well as one or more European languages. There are four major groups indigenous to Africa:

- The [*Afroasiatic*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afroasiatic_languages) languages are a language family of about 240 languages and 285 million people widespread throughout the Horn of Africa, North Africa, the [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel), and Southwest Asia.

- The [*Nilo-Saharan*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nilo-Saharan_languages) languages consist of a group of several possibly related [families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_family),

  [^cite_note-338]

  &#x20;spoken by 30 million people between 100 languages. Nilo-Saharan languages are spoken by ethnic groups in Chad, Ethiopia, Kenya, Nigeria, Sudan, South Sudan, Uganda, and northern Tanzania.

- The [*Niger-Congo*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger%E2%80%93Congo_languages) language family covers much of sub-Saharan Africa. In terms of number of languages, it is the largest language family in Africa and perhaps one of the largest in the world.

- The [*Khoisan*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoisan_languages) languages form a group of three unrelated

  [^cite_note-339]

  &#x20;families and two [isolates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_isolate) and number about fifty in total. They are mainly spoken in Southern Africa by approximately 400,000 people.

  [^cite_note-340]

  &#x20;Many of the Khoisan languages are [endangered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endangered_language). The Khoi and San peoples are considered the original inhabitants of this part of Africa.

Following the end of colonialism, nearly all countries adopted official languages that originated outside the continent, although several countries also granted legal recognition to indigenous languages (such as [Swahili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swahili_language), [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_language), [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_language) and [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_language)). In numerous countries, English and French (*see [African French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_French)*) are used for communication in the public sphere such as government, commerce, education and the media. Arabic, [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language), [Afrikaans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaans) and Spanish are examples of languages that trace their origin to outside of Africa, and that are used by millions of Africans today, both in the public and private spheres. Italian is spoken by some in former Italian colonies. German is spoken in Namibia, as it was a former German protectorate. At least one-fifth of Africans speak the former colonial languages.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344] Moreover, in recent years some countries have been considering removing their official former colonial languages, such as Mali, Burkina Faso and Niger which removed French as an official language in the 2020s in favour of native languages,[^cite_note-345] while also renaming colonial street names.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347]

### Health



Prevalence of HIV/AIDS in Africa, total (% of population ages 15–49), in 2011 ([World Bank](36752.md))&#x20;

| over 15%   5–15%   2–5%   1–2%   0.5–1%   0.1–0.5%   not available |
| --- |

More than 85% of individuals use [traditional medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_traditional_medicine) as an alternative to often expensive allopathic medical health care and costly pharmaceutical products. The OAU declared the 2000s as the African Decade on African traditional medicine in an effort to promote The WHO African Region's adopted resolution for institutionalising traditional medicine in health care systems.[^cite_note-348] Public policymakers in the region are challenged with consideration of the importance of traditional/indigenous health systems and whether their coexistence with the modern medical and health sub-sector would improve the equitability and accessibility of health care distribution, the health status of populations, and the social-economic development of nations within sub-Saharan Africa.[^cite_note-349]

[AIDS in post-colonial Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_Africa) is a prevalent issue. Although the continent is home to about 15.2 percent of the world's population,[^cite_note-350] more than two-thirds of the total infected worldwide—some 35 million people—were Africans, of whom 15 million have died.[^cite_note-Enc_p8-351] Sub-Saharan Africa alone accounted for an estimated 69 percent of all people living with HIV[^cite_note-2012_Facts-352] and 70 percent of all AIDS deaths in 2011.[^cite_note-dUNAIDSi_ck_2012-353] In sub-Saharan Africa, AIDS has raised death rates and lowered life expectancy among adults between ages 20 and 49 by about 20 years.[^cite_note-Enc_p8-351] Life expectancy has declined largely as a result of the HIV/AIDS epidemic, with life-expectancy in some countries reaching as low as 34 years.[^cite_note-354]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Africa)



The [Senegambian stone circles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senegambian_stone_circles), lying in The [Gambia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gambia) and [Senegal](27278.md), are a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Africa).

Africa is divided into a great number of ethnic cultures.[^cite_note-Khair_El-Din_Haseeb_et_al.-355][^cite_note-Halimrakat-356][^cite_note-Tajudeen_Abdul_Raheem_1996-357] Cultural regeneration has been an integral aspect of post-independence nation-building, with a recognition of the need to harness the cultural resources to enrich the process of education, requiring the creation of an enabling environment in several ways. In recent times, the call for a much greater emphasis on the cultural dimension in all aspects of development has become increasingly vocal.[^cite_note-358] Some aspects of traditional cultures have become less practised in recent years as a result of neglect and suppression by colonial and post-colonial regimes. For example, African customs were discouraged, and African languages were prohibited in mission schools,[^cite_note-pearsonhighered.com-359] while colonialists such as [Leopold II of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopold_II_of_Belgium) attempted to "[civilize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilizing_mission)" Africans by discouraging polygamy and [witchcraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witchcraft_in_Africa).[^cite_note-pearsonhighered.com-359]

Editors F. Abiola Irele and Simon Gikandi comment that the current identity of African literature had its genesis in the "traumatic encounter between Africa and Europe".[^cite_note-360] On the other hand, Mhoze Chikowero believes that Africans deployed music, dance, spirituality, and other performative cultures to (re)assert themselves as active agents and indigenous intellectuals, to unmake their colonial marginalisation and reshape their own destinies.[^cite_note-361]

As of March 2023, 98 African properties are listed by [UNESCO](21786641.md) as [World Heritage Sites](44940.md), with 54 cultural sites, 39 natural sites, and 5 mixed sites. The [List of World Heritage in Danger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_in_Danger) includes 15 African sites.[^cite_note-362]

### Visual art

These paragraphs are an excerpt from [African art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_art).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=African_art\&action=edit)]

[African art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_art) refers to works of [visual art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_arts), including works of sculpture, painting, [metalwork](266443.md), and [pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pottery), originating from the [various peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ethnic_groups_of_Africa) of the African continent and influenced by distinct, indigenous traditions of [aesthetic expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesthetics).

While the various artistic traditions of such a large and diverse continent display considerable regional and cultural variety, there are consistent artistic themes, recurring [motifs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motif_\(visual_arts\)), and unifying elements across the broad spectrum of the African visual expression.[^cite_note-363] As is the case for every [artistic tradition in human history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_history), African art was created within specific social, political, and religious contexts. Likewise, African art was often created not purely [for art's sake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_for_art's_sake), but rather with some practical, [spiritual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality), and/or [didactic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didactic_art) purpose in mind. In general, African art prioritizes conceptual and symbolic representation over [realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)), aiming to visualize the subject's spiritual essence.[^cite_note-African_art_\:8-364]

[Ethiopian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_art), heavily influenced by [Ethiopia](187749.md)'s long-standing [Christian tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Ethiopia),[^cite_note-365] is also different from most African art, where [Traditional African religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_African_religion) (with [Islam](6037917.md) prevalent in the north east and north west presently) was dominant until the 20th century.[^cite_note-African_art_nytimes-366] African art includes [prehistoric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory) and [ancient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_history) art, the Islamic art of [West Africa](67393.md), the [Christian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_art) of [East Africa](260987.md), and the traditional [artifacts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artifact_\(archaeology\)) of these and other regions. Many [African sculptures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_sculpture) were historically made of wood and other natural materials that have not survived from earlier than a few centuries ago, although rare older pottery and metal figures can be found in some areas.[^cite_note-367] Some of the earliest decorative objects, such as [shell beads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shell_jewelry) and evidence of paint, have been discovered in Africa, dating to the [Middle Stone Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Stone_Age).[^cite_note-African_art_Mitchell_2013_p._375-368][^cite_note-African_art_Henshilwood_et_al._2011-369][^cite_note-African_art_McBrearty_Brooks_2000-370]

[Masks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_African_masks) are important elements in the art of many people, along with human figures, and are often highly stylized. There exist diverse styles, which can often be observed within a single [context of origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_of_origin) and may be influenced by the intended use of the object. Nevertheless, broad regional trends are discernible. Sculpture is most common among "groups of settled cultivators in the areas drained by the [Niger](21373.md) and [Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_River) rivers" in West Africa.[^cite_note-African_art_Honour_&_Fleming,_557-371] Direct images of deities are relatively infrequent, but masks in particular are or were often made for ritual ceremonies. Since the late 19th century, there has been an increasing amount of [African art in Western collections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_art_in_Western_collections), the finest pieces of which are displayed as part of the history of [colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonization).

African art had an important influence on European [Modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism) art,[^cite_note-African_art_African_Influences_in_Modern_Art-372] which was inspired by their interest in abstract depiction.[^cite_note-African_art_\:8-364] It was this appreciation of African sculpture that has been attributed to the very concept of "African art", as seen by European and American artists and art historians.[^cite_note-373]

Obidoh Freeborn posits that colonialism is one element that has created the character of modern African art.[^cite_note-374] According to authors Douglas Fraser and Herbert M. Cole, "The precipitous alterations in the power structure wrought by colonialism were quickly followed by drastic iconographic changes in the art."[^cite_note-books.google.com-375] Fraser and Cole assert that in Igboland some art objects "lack the vigor and careful craftsmanship of the earlier art objects that served traditional functions".[^cite_note-books.google.com-375] Author Chika Okeke-Agulu states "the racist infrastructure of British imperial enterprise forced upon the political and cultural guardians of empire a denial and suppression of an emergent sovereign Africa and modernist art".[^cite_note-376]



[Nok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nok_culture) figure, Nigeria (5th century BCE–5th century CE)



Rock art at the [Laas Geel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laas_Geel) complex in [Somalia](27358.md), 3,500–2,500 BCE



Two [Benin Bronzes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_Bronze) from the 18th century

### Architecture

This section is an excerpt from [Architecture of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Africa).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Architecture_of_Africa\&action=edit)]











Clockwise from upper left: [Great Mosque of Djenne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Djenne) in [Mali](19127.md); [Kasubi Tombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kasubi_Tombs) in [Uganda](31816.md); Lideta Market in [Ethiopia](187749.md); Hikma Religious and Secular Complex in [Niger](21373.md); Zaria Emir's Palace [Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_of_Hausa_kingdoms) in [Nigeria](21383.md).

&#x20;Like other aspects of the [culture of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Africa), the [architecture of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Africa) is exceptionally diverse. Throughout the [history of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Africa), [Africans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_people) have developed their own local [architectural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture) traditions. In some cases, broader regional styles can be identified, such as the [Sudano-Sahelian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudano-Sahelian_architecture) of [West Africa](67393.md). A common theme in traditional African architecture is the use of [fractal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fractal) scaling: small parts of the structure tend to look similar to larger parts, such as a circular village made of circular houses.

[^cite_note-377]



The [Great Pyramids of Giza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giza_pyramid_complex) are regarded as one of the greatest architectural feats of all time and are one of the [Seven Wonders of the Ancient World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_the_Ancient_World).&#x20;

African architecture in some areas has been influenced by external cultures for centuries, according to available evidence. Western architecture has influenced coastal areas since the late 15th century and is now an important source of inspiration for many larger buildings, particularly in major cities.

African architecture uses a wide range of materials, including thatch, stick/wood, mud, [mudbrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudbrick), [rammed earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rammed_earth), and stone. These material preferences vary by region: North Africa for stone and rammed earth, the [Horn of Africa](62142.md) for stone and mortar, West Africa for mud/adobe, Central Africa for thatch/wood and more perishable materials, Southeast and Southern Africa for stone and thatch/wood.

### Cinema

This section is an excerpt from [Cinema of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Africa).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Cinema_of_Africa\&action=edit)]



Cinematic street poster in [Tunis](57659.md), [Tunisia](30188.md) for the Egyptian film *[Saladin the Victorious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saladin_the_Victorious)* (1963, Arabic: الناصر صلاح الدين, Al Nasser Salah Ad-Din) directed by [Youssef Chahine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youssef_Chahine) starring [Ahmed Mazhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Mazhar) as Saladin, [Salah Zulfikar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_Zulfikar), [Nadia Lutfi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadia_Lutfi) and others

[Cinema of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Africa) refers to the [film industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_industry) and [films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film) produced in the continent of Africa. It covers both the [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_film) and present of the [making](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filmmaking) or screening of films on the African continent, and also refers to the persons involved in this form of [audiovisual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audiovisual) culture. It dates back to the late 19th century, when film reels were the primary cinematic technology in use. [Cairo](6293.md) has been the capital of [film industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_industry) in Africa since the early 20th century to the present day.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380]

As there are more than 50 countries with audiovisual traditions, there is no one single 'African cinema'. Both historically and culturally, there are major regional differences between [North African](21714.md) and [sub-Saharan](27067.md) cinemas, and between the cinemas of different countries.[^cite_note-Cinema_of_Africa_Hayward,_Susan_2006._p._426-442-381] The [Egyptian film industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Egypt) and the [Tunisian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Tunisia) are also among the oldest in the world. Cinema of Egypt in particular is the most established and flourishing industry in Africa.[^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383] Pioneers [Auguste and Louis Lumière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_and_Louis_Lumi%C3%A8re) screened their films in [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), [Cairo](6293.md), [Tunis](57659.md), [Susa, Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susa,_Libya) and [Hammam-Lif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammam-Lif), [Tunisia](30188.md) in 1896.[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385] [Albert Samama Chikly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Samama_Chikly) is often cited as the first producer of indigenous African cinema, screening his own short documentaries in the [casino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casino) of Tunis as early as December 1905.[^cite_note-Cinema_of_Africa_Ritrovato-386]

### Music

This section is an excerpt from [Music of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Africa).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Music_of_Africa\&action=edit)]



[Umm Kulthum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Kulthum)

The [continent of Africa and its music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Africa) is vast and highly diverse, with different [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Africa) and [nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries) maintaining distinct [musical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music) traditions. African music includes genres such as makwaya, [highlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlife), [mbube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbube_\(genre\)), [township music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Township_music), [jùjú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B9j%C3%BA_music), [fuji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuji_music), [jaiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaiva), [afrobeat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrobeat), [afrofusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro_fusion), [mbalax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbalax), [Congolese rumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congolese_rumba), [soukous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soukous), [ndombolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ndombolo), [makossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makossa), [kizomba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kizomba), and [taarab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taarab), among others.[^cite_note-Music_of_Africa_\:0-387] African music also uses a wide variety of instruments from across the continent.

The music and dance traditions of the [African diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora), shaped to varying degrees by African musical traditions, include [American genres](171080.md) such as [Dixieland jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dixieland_jazz), [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues), and [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), as well as [Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean_music) styles such as [calypso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calypso_music) (see [kaiso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaiso)), and [soca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soca_music). [Latin American music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_music) genres including [cumbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumbia), [salsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salsa_music), [son cubano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_cubano), [rumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_rumba), [conga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conga_\(music\)), [bomba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bomba_\(Puerto_Rico\)), [samba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba), and [zouk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zouk_\(musical_movement\)) developed from the music of [enslaved Africans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Atlantic_slave_trade) and have, in turn, influenced contemporary [African popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_popular_music).[^cite_note-Music_of_Africa_\:0-387][^cite_note-Music_of_Africa_Columbia_University-388]



[Franco Luambo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco_Luambo)

### Dance

This section is an excerpt from [African dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_dance).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=African_dance\&action=edit)]

[African dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_dance) (also Afro dance, Afrodance and Afro-dance)[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393] refers to the various dance styles of [sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md). These dances are closely connected with the traditional [rhythms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhythm_in_Sub-Saharan_Africa) and [music traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-Saharan_African_music_traditions) of the region. Music and dancing is an integral part of many traditional African societies. Songs and dances facilitate teaching and promoting social values, the celebration of special events and major life milestones, the performance of [oral history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_history) and other recitations, and spiritual experiences.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalone19969-394] African dance uses the concepts of [polyrhythm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyrhythm) and total body articulation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsh-Asante200928-395] African dances are often a collective activity performed in large groups, with significant interaction between dancers and onlookers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsh-Asante200935-396]

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Africa)



Best results of African men's national football teams at the FIFA World Cup

Fifty-four African countries have football teams in the [Confederation of African Football](715265.md). Egypt has won the [African Cup](250680.md) seven times, and a record-making three times in a row. Cameroon, Nigeria, Morocco, Senegal, Ghana, and Algeria have advanced to the knockout stage of recent [FIFA World Cups](11370.md). Morocco at the [2022 World Cup in Qatar](17742072.md) was the first African nation to reach the semi-finals of the FIFA Men's World Cup. South Africa hosted the [2010 World Cup tournament](168079.md), becoming the first African country to do so. The top clubs in each football league play the [CAF Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CAF_Champions_League), while lower-ranked clubs compete in [CAF Confederation Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CAF_Confederation_Cup).

In recent years, the continent has progressed in terms of state-of-the-art basketball facilities, which have been built in cities such as [Cairo](6293.md), [Dakar](44251.md), [Johannesburg](58893.md), [Kigali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kigali), [Luanda](18402.md), and [Rades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rades).[^cite_note-397] The number of African basketball players who drafted into the U.S. [NBA](22093.md) experienced growth in the 2010s.[^cite_note-398]

Cricket is popular in some nations. [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_national_cricket_team) and [Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zimbabwe_national_cricket_team) have [Test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Test_cricket) status, while [Kenya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenya_national_cricket_team) is the leading non-test team and previously had [One-Day International cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Day_International) status (from [10 October 1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_Cup_1997-98), until [30 January 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Cricket_World_Cup_Qualifier#Super_Six)). The three countries jointly hosted the [2003 Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Cricket_World_Cup). [Namibia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namibia_national_cricket_team) also has played in a World Cup. Morocco hosted the [2002 Morocco Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Morocco_Cup), but [the national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morocco_national_cricket_team) has never qualified for a major tournament.

Rugby is popular in several southern African nations. Namibia and Zimbabwe have appeared on multiple occasions at the [Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_World_Cup), while South Africa is the most successful national team at the Rugby World Cup, having won the tournament on four occasions, in 1995, 2007, 2019, and 2023.[^cite_note-399]

[Traditional sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_traditional_games_in_Africa) were strictly marginalised during the colonial era, and many are dying or have gone extinct under the pressure of modernisation, however many remain popular despite not having formal governmental recognition or support.[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401]<sup>: 193–194 </sup> Some examples are [Senegalese wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senegalese_wrestling), [Dambe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dambe), [Nguni stick-fighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguni_stick-fighting), and [Savika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savika).
